⏺ Time Deposit Kata: analysis

  I haven't changed any files. mvn test passes today, but the only test checks
  nothing. Docker is not running on this machine, so Testcontainers tests won't
  run until it is started.

  Labels used below: [C] means I confirmed it from the code or README. [A] means
  it's an assumption I'd need to document.

  ---

  1. Requirements

  Functional (README.md)
  - Exactly two REST endpoints:
    - one that updates the balances of all time deposits;
    - one that returns all time deposits with the fields id, planType, balance,
      days and withdrawals.
  - Two database tables:
    - timeDeposits: id (int PK), planType (string), days (int), balance
      (decimal), all required.
    - withdrawals: id (int PK), timeDepositId (FK), amount (decimal), date
      (date), all required.
  - Interest rules:
    - Basic 1%, Student 3% (none after 1 year), Premium 5% (only after 45 days).
    - No plan earns interest in its first 30 days.

  Technical
  - Don't break the shared TimeDeposit class or change the updateBalance
    signature. updateBalance behaviour must stay the same.
  - The design should make future interest rules easy to add (extensibility),
    and follow SOLID and clean code.
  - Preferred stack: an OpenAPI/Swagger contract, hexagonal architecture, atomic
    commits and Testcontainers.
  - Handling invalid input and exceptions is not required.
  - Ambiguities should be resolved with sensible assumptions, explained in code
    comments.
  - AI documentation: the tools, the setup, the rules/prompts used, and which
    parts were AI-assisted.
  - Submission: instructions for calling the endpoints through Swagger, and a
    public fork.

  Current state [C]: the project is plain Java 17 + Maven. It has no Spring, no
  database, no API and no withdrawals model. pom.xml contains only JUnit 5.9.0
  and AssertJ 3.23.1, plus an old surefire plugin (3.0.0-M3).

  ---

  2. How interest is calculated today (TimeDepositCalculator.updateBalance, 
  lines 8–29) [C]

  For each deposit in the list, the method works out one month's interest and
  adds it to the balance:

  ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │  Plan (planType,    │     Earns interest when     │  Interest formula   │
  │     exact text)     │                             │    (all double)     │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ "basic"             │ days > 30, i.e. day 31      │ balance * 0.01 / 12 │
  │                     │ onwards                     │                     │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ "student"           │ days > 30 && days < 366,    │ balance * 0.03 / 12 │
  │                     │ i.e. days 31–365            │                     │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ "premium"           │ days > 30 && days > 45,     │ balance * 0.05 / 12 │
  │                     │ i.e. day 46 onwards         │                     │
  ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ anything else       │ never                       │ 0                   │
  └─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  Details that must stay exactly as they are:
  1. Rates are annual, applied monthly. Each rate is divided by 12. The
     calculation order balance * rate / 12 matters: balance * (rate/12) can give
     a slightly different double.
  2. Rounding happens only on the interest (line 26): new 
     BigDecimal(interest).setScale(2, HALF_UP).doubleValue().
     - new BigDecimal(double) uses the exact binary value of the number, not
       BigDecimal.valueOf. A value that looks like it ends in …5 but is stored
       as …4999 rounds down.
     - The rounded interest is then added to the balance as a double. The
       balance itself is never rounded, so it can pick up floating-point noise
       (e.g. …0.8100000001).
  3. The 30-day check runs first. The plan type is only compared when days > 30.
  4. Plan type matching is case-sensitive (equals). "Basic" or " basic" earn
     nothing, and so does an unknown plan.
  5. The deposit objects are changed in place. Only the balance changes; days is
     never incremented.
  6. The method is not idempotent. Calling it twice applies a second month of
     interest, compounded on the new balance, with the same days.
  7. Withdrawals play no part in the calculation.
  8. Null handling:
     - null balance: always throws a NullPointerException (the unboxing on
       line 26).
     - null planType: throws only when days > 30.
     - null list: throws.
     - empty list: does nothing.
  9. Negative balances give negative interest. HALF_UP rounds away from zero.

  Worked example: basic, 1,234,567.00, 45 days → interest 1028.8058… → rounded
  to 1028.81 → new balance ≈ 1,235,595.81. This is the case the existing test
  runs, but it never checks the result.

  ---

  3. Code smells and architectural weaknesses

  TimeDepositCalculator
  - Indexed loop calling xs.get(i) repeatedly, and unclear names (xs, a2d).
  - Nested if/else if on strings. Adding a plan means editing this method, which
    breaks the open/closed principle.
  - Magic values everywhere: 30, 45, 366, 0.01, 0.03, 0.05, 12, and the plan
    names.
  - Money is held in double, and the code mixes double with BigDecimal only for
    rounding.
  - Unknown plans are silently ignored.

  TimeDeposit
  - Balance is a boxed Double (nullable).
  - Fields that never change aren't final, and there's no toString, equals or
    hashCode.
  - It has no withdrawals, even though the GET response needs them.
  - It is "shared", so its public API is frozen.

  TimeDepositCalculatorTest
  - assertThat(1).isEqualTo(1) checks nothing, so there are effectively zero 
    tests.

  Structure
  - Everything is in one package with no layers.
  - No persistence, API or application layer exists yet.

  Build
  - Old surefire plugin.
  - The Spring Boot, database and OpenAPI dependencies are all still to be
    added.
  - time-deposit-kata.iml (an IntelliJ project file) is committed to git. That's
    minor.

  ---

  4. Missing tests

  Characterization tests (tests that pin down current behaviour before
  refactoring), to add first and parameterized where it makes sense:

  Day boundaries
  - basic: 30 (no interest) / 31 (interest)
  - student: 30 / 31 / 365 (interest) / 366 (none)
  - premium: 30 / 45 (none) / 46 (interest)
  - day 0

  Plan type
  - unknown plan → balance unchanged
  - wrong case ("Basic") → unchanged

  Rounding
  - interest under half a cent rounds to 0 (e.g. 1.00 basic)
  - an exact .xx5 case (e.g. 6.00 basic → 0.005 → 0.01)
  - a value whose binary form sits just below .xx5, which fails if anyone
    "fixes" the code to use BigDecimal.valueOf. I'll find concrete values when I
    write the test.

  Amounts
  - zero balance
  - negative balance
  - very large balance (where double loses precision)

  Repeated updates
  - two calls → interest compounds on the updated balance, and days stays the
    same
  - a deposit that isn't eligible stays unchanged however many times it runs

  List handling
  - several deposits in one list are updated independently
  - empty list
  - id, planType and days are never changed

  Later tests
  - persistence adapter against Postgres via Testcontainers
  - web layer (MockMvc) for both endpoints, checked against the OpenAPI contract
  - one end-to-end test: seed → POST update → GET → expected balances and
    withdrawals

  ---

  5. Ambiguities and assumptions to document

  #: 1
  Ambiguity: Is "1% interest" annual or monthly?
  Proposed assumption: Annual rate, divided by 12 each month. The code is the
    source of truth [C].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Ambiguity: "1 year" for Student
  Proposed assumption: 365 days: interest through day 365, none from day 366
    [C: < 366].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Ambiguity: "Interest starts after 45 days" / "first 30 days"
  Proposed assumption: Strictly greater than: day 46 / day 31 [C].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Ambiguity: Who increments days?
  Proposed assumption: Not the update endpoint. days is stored data [A]. The
    update applies exactly one month's interest.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Ambiguity: Calling the update endpoint repeatedly
  Proposed assumption: Each call applies interest again, not idempotent [C in
    the calculator]. Adding a guard would change behaviour, so I'll document
    it instead [A].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Ambiguity: Shape of withdrawals in GET
  Proposed assumption: An array of {id, amount, date} per deposit [A].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Ambiguity: Do withdrawals affect interest?
  Proposed assumption: No [C]. They're only part of the read model.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Ambiguity: Decimal precision in the database
  Proposed assumption: NUMERIC(19,2) [A]. Converting double ↔ BigDecimal
    happens in the persistence adapter with BigDecimal.valueOf, which strips
    floating-point noise. That rounding happens in storage, not in the
    calculator.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 9
  Ambiguity: Table names are camelCase (timeDeposits)
  Proposed assumption: Keep them exactly, quoted in SQL [A].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 10
  Ambiguity: Plan type values
  Proposed assumption: Lowercase basic/student/premium stored as given.
    Unknown values earn nothing [C].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 11
  Ambiguity: HTTP design of the update endpoint
  Proposed assumption: POST /time-deposits/balance-updates → 204 [A]. The
    README gives no verb or path.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 12
  Ambiguity: Initial data
  Proposed assumption: A Flyway seed migration with sample rows, so Swagger
    can be tried immediately [A].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 13
  Ambiguity: Two update calls running at the same time
  Proposed assumption: Out of scope. Each update runs in one transaction [A].
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 14
  Ambiguity: Can TimeDeposit gain a withdrawals field?
  Proposed assumption: Avoid it. Keep TimeDeposit byte-for-byte unchanged and
    put withdrawals in the read model [A, conservative].

  ---

  6. Refactoring plan

  One step per commit, with tests green after each:

  1. Characterization tests for everything in §4, replacing the empty test. No
     production change.
  2. Clean up updateBalance internally: for-each loop, clear names, named
     constants. The signature and the arithmetic stay identical.
  3. Interest policies (the extensibility point):
     - Create an InterestPolicy interface: double monthlyInterest(TimeDeposit).
       Implement it in BasicInterestPolicy, StudentInterestPolicy and
       PremiumInterestPolicy, each keeping its own checks (including the 30-day
       rule) and the exact balance * rate / 12 expression.
     - The calculator looks up the policy by plan type in a Map<String, 
       InterestPolicy>, with zero interest as the default. That keeps unknown
       and wrong-case plans earning nothing.
     - The calculator still does the rounding exactly as on line 26.
     - Why a string-keyed map and not an enum: an enum valueOf would throw on
       unknown plans, which changes behaviour.
  4. Add Spring Boot, a Flyway schema and seed, and Postgres. No endpoints yet.
  5. Persistence adapter behind a TimeDepositRepository interface (the output
     port), with Testcontainers tests.
  6. Application services: UpdateAllBalances (load → calculator → save, in one
     transaction) and GetAllTimeDeposits.
  7. OpenAPI contract and controller for the two endpoints, with web-layer
     tests.
  8. End-to-end test, dependency review, README (Swagger usage, assumptions),
     and an AI_USAGE.md.

  Trade-off on step 3: a 30-day check inside each policy repeats a little code.
  In return, a future plan can have a different starting window, which is what
  the README's wording "for any existing plans" hints at. Alternatively the
  calculator could keep the 30-day check globally, which is simpler but less
  flexible.

  ---

  7. Proposed architecture

  org.ikigaidigital
  ├── TimeDeposit, TimeDepositCalculator      ← domain; package and class names 
  unchanged ("shared")
  ├── domain/interest/                         ← InterestPolicy + 3 
  implementations; Withdrawal record
  ├── application/                             ← UpdateAllBalancesService, 
  GetAllTimeDepositsService,
  │                                              TimeDepositRepository (output 
  port interface)
  └── adapter/
      ├── in/web/                              ← controller implementing the 
  generated API interface, DTO mapping
      └── out/persistence/                     ← SQL adapter, conversion to/from
  double

  - The only interface is the output port (TimeDepositRepository), plus
    InterestPolicy. Both have a concrete reason to exist. The use cases are
    plain service classes with no input-port interfaces, which would add nothing
    here.
  - Stack: Spring Boot 3.x, Java 17, PostgreSQL, Flyway, Testcontainers
    (Postgres), JUnit 5 and AssertJ. I'll pin exact versions when I edit
    pom.xml.
  - Read model: GET returns a TimeDeposit together with its List<Withdrawal> and
    is mapped to the API DTO. TimeDeposit stays untouched.

  ---

  Decisions I need from you before I start

  1. OpenAPI: contract-first (hand-written openapi.yaml +
     openapi-generator-maven-plugin, which fits the README's "contract" wording;
     my pick) or code-first (springdoc annotations; less build setup)?
  2. Database access: JdbcClient with plain SQL (my pick: two tables, quoted
     camelCase names, full control over loading withdrawals) or Spring Data JPA
     (less SQL, heavier, more mapping)?
  3. The 30-day rule: inside each policy (my pick) or a global check in the
     calculator?
  4. Update endpoint: is POST /time-deposits/balance-updates → 204 OK?

  I haven't verified exact Spring Boot and plugin versions yet. The worked
  example (1,235,595.81) is calculated by hand, not taken from a test run.
  Testcontainers needs Docker running.

⏺ I recorded both decisions in docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md and updated every place
  in the doc that depended on them.

  - OpenAPI, code-first: the API contract will be generated from the
    controller's annotations by springdoc-openapi. It's served at /v3/api-docs,
    with Swagger UI on top. There's no hand-written YAML file and no generator
    plugin.
  - Spring Data JPA:
    - Separate entities: the database classes are kept separate from the domain
      TimeDeposit, so that shared class stays untouched.
    - Schema: Flyway creates it and Hibernate only checks it.
    - Naming: the camelCase table and column names (timeDeposits, planType,
      timeDepositId) need explicit mapping. Otherwise Hibernate renames them to
      snake_case.
    - Loading: withdrawals are loaded in the same query as their deposits, so
      each deposit doesn't trigger an extra query.

  Decisions 3 and 4 are still marked as proposed in the doc:
  - 30-day rule: inside each plan's interest rule.
  - Update endpoint: POST /time-deposits/balance-updates, returning 204.

  Confirm or change those two, and I'll start step 1 of the plan:
  characterization tests only, with no production code changes.

⏺ 32 tests pass. To check that they actually catch regressions, I'll temporarily
  make two likely bad changes to the calculator, run the tests, and restore it
  from git:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All four decisions are now marked as decided in docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md, and
  step 1 is done. TimeDepositCalculatorTest now has 32 tests that pin down how
  the calculator behaves today, and they all pass. The calculator code itself is
  unchanged.

  What the tests cover:
  - Day boundaries: basic 0/30/31; student 30/31/365/366; premium 30/31/45/46.
  - Plan names: unknown or differently-cased names (gold, Basic, ' student',
    empty) earn no interest.
  - Rounding:
    - interest under half a cent rounds to 0;
    - 6.00 basic rounds up by half-up;
    - zero and negative balances;
    - only the interest is rounded, never the balance.
  - Binary rounding: two cases where the stored number sits just below half a
    cent. For example, 18.00 basic gives interest 0.01499999…, which today
    rounds to 0.01. "Fixing" this with BigDecimal.valueOf would give 0.02, and
    these tests would fail.
  - Repeated updates: two calls compound the interest and leave days unchanged.
    A deposit that doesn't qualify stays the same after several calls.
  - Several deposits in one list are each updated on their own. An empty list
    works, and only the balance ever changes.
  - The README's original example: 1,234,567.00 → 1,235,595.81, now confirmed by
    a test rather than by hand.

  I took the expected values from running the current calculator. As a check, I
  temporarily made two likely mistakes: switching to BigDecimal.valueOf, and
  changing the student limit to <= 366. Both made tests fail. I then restored
  the calculator from git, so it has no changes.

  Other changes:
  - pom.xml: I added junit-jupiter-params 5.9.0, test scope only, for the
    table-style tests.
  - Javadoc: the test class has a class-level Javadoc. The test methods are
    package-private, so the public-method Javadoc rule doesn't apply to them.

  Not tested: null inputs. The README says invalid input doesn't need handling,
  and the analysis doc already describes what happens.

  Nothing is committed yet. Should I commit step 1 ("Add characterization tests
  for TimeDepositCalculator"), or go straight on to step 2 (internal cleanup of
  updateBalance)?

          -ance * 0.03 / 12` |

      137 +| R1 | The rates are annual and are divided by 12: one call ap
          +plies **one month** of interest. | [T] e.g. 10000.00 basic → 1
          +0008.33 |                                                     
      138 +| R2 | Matching the plan type is **case-sensitive and exact**:
          + `"Basic"`, `"PREMIUM"`, `" student"` and `""` earn nothing. |

      140 +| R4 | **Rounding starts from the exact binary value of the `d
          +ouble`** (`new BigDecimal(double)`). For example, 18.00 basic 
          +gives interest `0.01499999…`, which becomes 0.01. `BigDecimal.
          +valueOf` would give 0.02. | [T] same test (18.00 basic, 25.20

      141 +| R5 | **The balance itself is never rounded.** Its extra deci
          +mals are kept. | [T] `doesNotRoundTheBalanceItself` |         
      142 +| R6 | **Negative balances** get negative interest; `HALF_UP` 
          +rounds away from zero (−6.00 → −6.01). | [T] rounding test |  
      143 +| R7 | The sum is calculated in `double`. Very large balances 
          +keep only `double` precision (1e15 → 1.0008333333333334E15). |
          + [T] `keepsDoublePrecisionForVeryLargeBalances` |             
      144 +| R8 | The expression order is `balance * rate / 12`. Rewritin
          +g it as `balance * (rate / 12)` *may* change the last bit of t
          +he result. | [C] code; the effect is not demonstrated by a tes
          +t |                                                           
      145 
       62 -Behavior that must be preserved:                              
       63 -1. **Annual rates are applied monthly** (divided by 12). The c
          -alculation order `balance * rate / 12` matters: `balance * (ra
          -te / 12)` can give a different `double`.                      
       64 -2. **Only the interest is rounded** (line 26): `new BigDecimal
          -(interest).setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP).doubleValue()`.  
       65 -   - `new BigDecimal(double)` uses the **exact binary value**,
          - unlike `BigDecimal.valueOf`. A value that looks like `.xx5` b
          -ut is stored as `.xx4999…` rounds **down**.                   
       66 -   - The rounded interest is added to the balance as a `double
          -`. The balance itself is never rounded, so it can pick up floa
          -ting-point noise.                                             
       67 -3. **The 30-day check is evaluated first.** The plan type is o
          -nly compared when `days > 30`.                                
       68 -4. **Plan matching is case-sensitive** (`String.equals`). `"Ba
          -sic"` and unknown plans earn nothing.                         
       69 -5. **Deposits are changed in place.** Only the balance changes
          -; `days` is never incremented.                                
       70 -6. **Not idempotent.** Each call applies another month of inte
          -rest, compounded on the updated balance, with the same `days`.
       71 -7. **Withdrawals do not affect the calculation.**             
       72 -8. **Null handling:**                                         
       73 -   - `null` balance → always `NullPointerException` (unboxing 
          -on line 26);                                                  
       74 -   - `null` planType → NPE only when `days > 30`;             
       75 -   - `null` list → NPE;                                       
       76 -   - empty list → nothing happens.                            
       77 -9. **Negative balances** give negative interest. `HALF_UP` rou
          -nds away from zero.                                           
      146 +### 3.3 State and side effects                                
      147 
       79 -Worked example (calculated by hand): `basic`, balance `1234567
          -.00`, `days = 45` → interest `1028.8058…` → rounded `1028.81` 
          -→ new balance ≈ `1235595.81`.                                 
      148 +| # | Rule | Evidence |                                       
      149 +|---|---|---|                                                 
      150 +| S1 | Deposits are updated **in place**. Only `balance` chang
          +es; `id`, `planType` and `days` stay the same. | [T] `changesO
          +nlyTheBalance` |                                              
      151 +| S2 | `days` is **never incremented** by the calculator. | [T
          +] `repeatedUpdatesCompoundInterestWithoutChangingDays` |      
      152 +| S3 | **Not idempotent:** each call adds another month, compo
          +unded on the new balance (10000.00 → 10008.33 → 10016.67). | [
          +T] same test |                                                
      153 +| S4 | A deposit that isn't eligible stays unchanged after any
          + number of calls. | [T] `repeatedUpdatesLeaveIneligibleDeposit
          +Unchanged` |                                                  
      154 +| S5 | Each deposit in the list is calculated on its own. | [T
          +] `updatesEachDepositInTheListIndependently` |                
      155 +| S6 | An empty list is a no-op. | [T] `acceptsAnEmptyList` | 
      156 +| S7 | Withdrawals play no part: `TimeDeposit` has no withdraw
          +als. | [C] |                                                  
      157 +| S8 | The README example `basic`, 1234567.00, 45 days → 12355
          +95.81. | [T] `updatesTheOriginalExampleDeposit` |             
      158 
       81 -### `TimeDepositCalculatorTest`

          -o(1)`. **It checks nothing.** The calculator effectively has n
          -o tests.

          -s editing this method (breaks the open/closed principle);     
       92 -  - magic numbers and strings (`30`, `45`, `366`, `0.01`, `0.0
          -3`, `0.05`, `12`, plan names);

          -ed only for rounding;

      111 -### Risks                                                     
      112 -- **Rounding regression.** A well-meant "fix" (`BigDecimal.val
          -ueOf`, rounding the whole balance, or reordering the formula) 
          -would silently change results.                                
      113 -- **Boundary regression** at day 30/31, 45/46 and 365/366.    
      114 -- **Repeated updates.** The update endpoint applies interest o
          -n every call. Calling it twice, or concurrently, double-applie
          -s interest.                                                   
      115 -- **Precision at the database boundary.** Converting between `
          -double` (domain) and `DECIMAL` (database) can round or add flo
          -ating-point noise.                                            
      116 -- **"Shared" `TimeDeposit`.** Any change to its public API mig
          -ht break unseen consumers.                                    
      117 -- **Environment.** Testcontainers needs a running Docker daemo
          -n.                                                            
      194 +### 4.4 Risks                                                 
      195 
      196 +| # | Risk | Mitigation |                                     
      197 +|---|---|---|                                                 
      198 +| K1 | **Rounding regression:** a well-meant "fix" (`BigDecima
          +l.valueOf`, rounding the balance, reordering the formula) sile
          +ntly changes results. | R3–R7 are pinned by tests. R8 is not p
          +inned; keep the expression exactly as written. |              
      199 +| K2 | **Boundary regression** at day 30/31, 45/46 and 365/366
          +. | Pinned by tests. |                                        
      200 +| K3 | **Repeated updates:** the update endpoint applies inter
          +est on every call; two calls, or two at the same time, double-
          +apply it. | Documented [A]; see §6, A5 and A13. |             
      201 +| K4 | **Database precision:** converting between `double` (do
          +main) and `DECIMAL` (database) can round or add floating-point
          + noise. | Conversion handled in the persistence adapter only; 
          +covered by a Testcontainers round-trip test (planned). |      
      202 +| K5 | **The "shared" `TimeDeposit`:** changing its public API
          + could break consumers we can't see. | Keep it unchanged [A]. 
          +|                                                             
      203 +| K6 | **Environment:** Testcontainers needs a running Docker 
          +daemon; Docker was not running at analysis time. | Note it in 
          +the README. |

      123 -### Characterization tests for `updateBalance` (to write befor

      124 -- **Day boundaries:**                                         
      125 -  - basic: 30 (no interest) / 31 (interest);                  
      126 -  - student: 30 / 31 / 365 (interest) / 366 (none);           
      127 -  - premium: 30 / 45 (none) / 46 (interest);                  
      128 -  - day 0.                                                    
      129 -- **Plan type:**                                              
      130 -  - unknown plan → balance unchanged;                         
      131 -  - wrong case (`"Basic"`) → unchanged.                       
      132 -- **Rounding:**                                               
      133 -  - interest below half a cent rounds to 0 (e.g. `1.00` basic)
          -;                                                             
      134 -  - an exact half-cent case (e.g. `6.00` basic → `0.005` → `0.
          -01`);                                                         
      135 -  - **a value whose binary representation sits just below `.xx
          -5`**. This protects the `new BigDecimal(double)` behavior; con
          -crete values will be found while writing the test.            
      136 -- **Amounts:**                                                
      137 -  - zero balance;                                             
      138 -  - negative balance;                                         
      139 -  - very large balance (where `double` loses precision).      
      140 -- **Repeated updates:**                                       
      141 -  - two calls → interest compounds on the updated balance, and
          - `days` is unchanged;                                         
      142 -  - a deposit that isn't eligible stays unchanged after severa
          -l calls.                                                      
      143 -- **List handling:**                                          
      144 -  - several deposits in one list are updated independently;   
      145 -  - empty list;                                               
      146 -  - `id`, `planType` and `days` are never changed.

           sitory (output port interface)

          +IMAL)

      291 
      292  - **Domain:** no dependency on any framework. `TimeDeposit` an
           d `TimeDepositCalculator` keep their package, class names and
           public API.
     ...
      295  - **Stack:** Spring Boot 3.x (Java 17), Spring Data JPA, sprin
           gdoc-openapi, PostgreSQL, Flyway, Testcontainers (PostgreSQL),
            JUnit 5, AssertJ. Exact versions will be pinned when `pom.xml
           ` is changed.
      296  - **Money:** the legacy `double` calculation is preserved. `Bi
           gDecimal` is used at the API and persistence boundaries.

      220 -### Decisions                                                 
      221 -1. **OpenAPI: code-first** (decided by the author).           
      222 -   - springdoc-openapi generates the contract from the control
          -ler and its annotations.                                      
      223 -   - Swagger UI and `/v3/api-docs` are the published contract.
      224 -   - No hand-written YAML, no generator plugin.               
      225 -2. **Database access: Spring Data JPA** (decided by the author
          -).                                                            
      226 -   - JPA entities (`TimeDepositEntity`, `WithdrawalEntity`) li
          -ve in the persistence adapter, separate from the domain `TimeD
          -eposit`, so the shared class gets no JPA annotations.         
      227 -   - The schema is owned by Flyway; Hibernate only validates i
          -t (`ddl-auto=validate`).                                      
      228 -   - The camelCase table and column names (`timeDeposits`, `pl
          -anType`, `timeDepositId`) must be mapped explicitly. Hibernate
          -'s default naming strategy would turn them into snake_case.   
      229 -   - Withdrawals are fetched together with their deposits (fet
          -ch join / `@EntityGraph`), to avoid running one query per depo
          -sit (the N+1 problem).                                        
      230 -3. **The 30-day rule:** inside each policy (decided by the aut
          -hor).                                                         
      231 -4. **Update endpoint:** `POST /time-deposits/balance-updates` 
          -→ `204` (decided by the author).                              
      298 +### Decisions (all made by the author)                        
      299 +1. **OpenAPI: code-first.** springdoc-openapi generates the co
          +ntract from the controller and its annotations; Swagger UI and
          + `/v3/api-docs` are the published contract.                   
      300 +2. **Database access: Spring Data JPA.**                      
      301 +   - The JPA entities (`TimeDepositEntity`, `WithdrawalEntity`
          +) are separate from the domain `TimeDeposit`, so the shared cl
          +ass gets no JPA annotations.                                  
      302 +   - Flyway owns the schema; Hibernate only validates it (`ddl
          +-auto=validate`).                                             
      303 +   - The camelCase names are mapped explicitly.               
      304 +   - Withdrawals are fetched together with their deposits (fet
          +ch join / `@EntityGraph`), to avoid one query per deposit (the
          + N+1 problem).

  - Dependencies: the only test dependency I added is junit-jupiter-params 5.9.0
    (from step 1), which the parameterized tests need.
  - Docs: docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md is updated with the new test count and cases.

  Unexpected behavior (kept as is and pinned by tests)

  1. The balance can come out with a long tail of decimals. Student with balance
     1234567.89 gives 1237654.3099999998, not 1237654.31. The interest is
     rounded correctly to 3086.42, but adding it to the balance as a double
     introduces the error. This will matter when balances are saved to a
     2-decimal database column, which will quietly round them back to .31. I'll
     treat that as a database-boundary conversion and cover it with an
     integration test.
  2. Half-cent amounts don't always round up. 0.015 is stored in binary as
     0.01499999…, so it rounds down. For example, 18.00 basic gets 0.01 interest
     and premium 25.20 gets 0.10. The rounding still looks like normal half-up
     for 6.00 basic, which gets 0.01.
  3. The update is not idempotent. Each call adds another month of interest, and
     days never changes. So calling the update endpoint twice pays interest
     twice.
  4. Plan names must match exactly, including case. Basic, PREMIUM and  student
     all earn nothing, with no error.
  5. Premium's 30-day check is redundant, because premium already requires more
     than 45 days. This changes nothing.

  Ambiguities (documented in docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md)

  - "1 year" for the student plan is treated as 365 days: day 365 earns
    interest, day 366 doesn't.
  - "After 30 / 45 days" means strictly greater than.
  - The 3% and 5% rates are annual, divided by 12 each month.

  Not tested: null inputs, which the README says don't need handling. Nothing is
  committed yet. The next step is step 2 (internal cleanup), once you approve.

❯ Refactor the existing TimeDepositCalculator and TimeDeposit classes following 
  Clean Code principles.                                                        
                                                                                
  The characterization tests have already been committed and establish the      
  baseline behavior.                                                            
                                                                                
  Scope:                                                                        
  - Improve readability and maintainability.                                    
  - Use meaningful names for variables, methods and parameters.                 
  - Reduce nested conditionals and unnecessary complexity.                      
  - Extract small, cohesive methods where this genuinely improves clarity.      
  - Eliminate duplication and magic numbers where appropriate.                  
  - Follow the Single Responsibility Principle without over-engineering.        
  - Prefer straightforward, idiomatic Java over clever abstractions.            
                                                                                
  Strict constraints:                                                           
  - Preserve all existing observable behavior, including eligibility            
  boundaries, floating-point arithmetic, rounding, in-place mutation and        
  repeated updates.                                                             
  - Do not modify or weaken the characterization tests.                         
  - Do not introduce new frameworks, persistence, REST endpoints or             
  architectural layers yet.                                                     
  - Do not introduce Strategy patterns, policy abstractions or additional       
  classes unless clearly necessary for readability.                             
  - Avoid changing public APIs unless necessary and explicitly justified.       
  - Do not replace double with BigDecimal or alter the existing calculation     
  semantics.                                                                    
  - Keep changes small and focused.                                             
                                                                                
  Workflow:                                                                     
  1. Review the production code and existing tests.                             
  2. Identify concrete readability and maintainability problems.                
  3. Propose a minimal refactoring plan before editing.                         
  4. Implement the refactoring incrementally.                                   
  5. Run relevant tests after each meaningful change.                           
  6. Review the final diff for unnecessary changes.                             
  7. Run the full test suite and summarize what improved and why.               
                                                                                
  Do not commit changes. I will review and commit them manually.

         +e annualRate) {

⏺ I refactored both classes and the full suite still passes: 43 tests, 0
  failures. The behavior is unchanged, verified three ways:
  - Tests: all 43 characterization tests pass, and none of them changed.
  - Same results as the original: I compared the new calculator with the
    original from git on 2,000,000 random deposits, including repeated updates
    and negative and very large balances. Every result matched bit for bit. A
    null plan type also fails in the same cases as before.
  - Public API: the public constructors and methods of both classes are
    identical before and after (javap).

  Only those two production files changed. Nothing is committed.

  TimeDepositCalculator

  Before: for (int i…) with xs.get(i) repeated 11 times
  After: for (TimeDeposit deposit : deposits)
  Why: Says what it means; no index noise.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Before: Three nested ifs with interest += on a zero start value
  After: monthlyInterest(deposit): return 0 early for the first 30 days, then
    a switch on plan type
  Why: One level of logic; each plan's rule fits on one line.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Before: balance * rate / 12 copied three times
  After: monthlyInterest(balance, annualRate)
  Why: One formula, with a comment explaining why the operation order must
    stay.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Before: Rounding hidden inside the a2d line
  After: roundToCents(amount)
  Why: The non-obvious new BigDecimal(double) choice now has its reason
    written next to it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Before: 30, 45, 366, 0.01, 0.03, 0.05, 12, 2
  After: Named constants, e.g. STUDENT_LAST_INTEREST_DAY = 365
  Why: days <= 365 is the same as < 366 for whole days, and reads like the "1
    year" rule.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Before: xs, a2d
  After: deposits, interest
  Why: Meaningful names.

  Points that keep the behavior identical:
  - Order of checks: the 30-day check still runs before the plan type is
    examined, so a null plan type still fails only after day 30.
  - Plan names: the switch on strings matches them case-sensitively, exactly
    like the old equals.
  - Unknown plans and mutation: unknown plans still earn 0, and deposits are
    still updated in place.

  TimeDeposit

  - final fields: id, planType and days are now final. They had no setters, so
    nothing outside the class notices.
  - Formatting: the getters are formatted consistently.
  - Javadoc: both classes and all their public methods now have Javadoc, as
    CLAUDE.md requires.

  Left out on purpose

  - equals/hashCode/toString: adding equals would change how deposits compare to
    each other.
  - Balance type: it stays the boxed Double; changing it would break the shared
    API.
  - Plan-name constants and policy classes: policies come in the planned step 3.

  The rule that a deposit's 30-day check sits inside each plan (your decision 3)
  is also left for step 3. For now it remains one shared early return.

⏺ Plan: add a PlanType enum. TimeDeposit keeps its String planType, because it's
  part of the public API. The calculator turns the string into the enum through

⏺ I added a PlanType enum with BASIC, STUDENT and PREMIUM, and the calculator
  now uses it instead of raw strings. All 53 tests pass: the 43 characterization
  tests, which I didn't touch, plus 10 new PlanTypeTest tests. Results still
  match the original calculator bit for bit on 2,000,000 random deposits.

  Changes:
  - PlanType.java (new): each enum value carries its exact code ("basic",
    "student", "premium"). fromCode(String) returns Optional<PlanType>:
    - the match is case-sensitive, so "Basic" and "BASIC" find nothing;
    - an unknown code gives an empty result rather than an exception (valueOf
      would throw), so unknown plans still earn 0;
    - null still throws NullPointerException, the same as the old calculator did
      after day 30.
  - TimeDepositCalculator: the code switches on the enum and has no default
    branch. A new PlanType value won't compile until it gets an interest rule,
    and the plan names are now written in exactly one place.
  - PlanTypeTest (new): checks the known codes, unknown and wrong-case codes,
    and null.
  - docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md: the step-3 note now says interest policies will be
    looked up by PlanType instead of by string.

  TimeDeposit keeps String planType. Changing its constructor or getPlanType()
  would break the shared API. So a mistyped plan can still come in as a string
  from outside, for example from the database. The enum stops mistypes in our
  own code; it can't stop bad data, which still earns 0 as before.

  Nothing is committed.

⏺ PlanType now holds each plan's annual rate and number of interest-free days:

  BASIC("basic", 0.01, 30),
  STUDENT("student", 0.03, 30),
  PREMIUM("premium", 0.05, 45);

  All 53 tests pass, and the 43 characterization tests are untouched. Valid
  input still gives bit-identical results on 2,000,000 random deposits.

  Calculator changes:
  - Constants removed: the rate and interest-free-day constants are gone from
    the calculator, which now reads them from the enum.
  - Interest flow: monthlyInterest(deposit) looks up the plan, checks
    earnsInterest, and applies balance * annualRate / 12. Unknown plans still
    give 0.
  - Eligibility: earnsInterest handles each plan's interest-free days plus one
    student-only rule: no interest after day 365. I left
    STUDENT_LAST_INTEREST_DAY in the calculator because you asked only for the
    rate and the free days. It could move into the enum as a nullable "last
    interest day" field if you'd like every plan rule in one place.

  One behavior change, for invalid input only: a null plan type now always
  throws NullPointerException. Before, it threw only after day 30, because the
  30-day check ran before the plan comparison. Now the plan has to be looked up
  first to know its free days. The README says invalid input doesn't need
  handling, so I accepted the change. It's recorded as N2 in
  docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md.

  This refactoring makes the planned step 3 (separate InterestPolicy classes)
  mostly unnecessary. The rates and free days now live in the enum, and the only
  rule specific to one plan is the student 365-day limit. I'd drop the policy
  classes unless the rules become more varied later. Nothing is committed.

❯ Refactor the existing TimeDepositCalculator and TimeDeposit classes following 
  Clean Code principles.                                                        
                                                                                
  The characterization tests have already been committed and establish the      
  baseline behavior.                                                            
                                                                                
  Scope:                                                                        
  - Improve readability and maintainability.                                    
  - Use meaningful names for variables, methods and parameters.                 
  - Reduce nested conditionals and unnecessary complexity.                      
  - Extract small, cohesive methods where this genuinely improves clarity.      
  - Eliminate duplication and magic numbers where appropriate.                  
  - Follow the Single Responsibility Principle without over-engineering.        
  - Prefer straightforward, idiomatic Java over clever abstractions.            
                                                                                
  Strict constraints:                                                           
  - Preserve all existing observable behavior, including eligibility            
  boundaries, floating-point arithmetic, rounding, in-place mutation and        
  repeated updates.                                                             
  - Do not modify or weaken the characterization tests.                         
  - Do not introduce new frameworks, persistence, REST endpoints or             
  architectural layers yet.                                                     
  - Do not introduce Strategy patterns, policy abstractions or additional       
  classes unless clearly necessary for readability.                             
  - Avoid changing public APIs unless necessary and explicitly justified.       
  - Do not replace double with BigDecimal or alter the existing calculation     
  semantics.                                                                    
  - Keep changes small and focused.                                             
                                                                                
  Workflow:                                                                     
  1. Review the production code and existing tests.                             
  2. Identify concrete readability and maintainability problems.                
  3. Propose a minimal refactoring plan before editing.                         
  4. Implement the refactoring incrementally.                                   
  5. Run relevant tests after each meaningful change.                           
  6. Review the final diff for unnecessary changes.                             
  7. Run the full test suite and summarize what improved and why.               
                                                                                
  Do not commit changes. I will review and commit them manually.

  One deviation, which this request's "preserve all observable behavior" rules 
  out: a null plan type now throws at every age, where before it threw only
  after day 30. It comes from moving the interest-free days into PlanType at
  your request: the plan has to be looked up before the days can be checked. It
  only affects invalid input, which the README excludes, and it's documented as
  N2 in docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md.

  If you want the old behavior back, the calculator would need one global 30-day
  check before the plan lookup. That brings back a duplicated 30, which the
  enum fields were meant to remove. My recommendation is to keep the current
  version.

  Small thing I noticed and left alone: there are two private methods named
  monthlyInterest, one taking a deposit and one taking a balance and rate.
  Renaming the second to monthlyInterestOn(balance, annualRate) would be a
  slight improvement, but not enough to justify more churn.

  Ready for your review and commit.

⏺ I refactored earnsInterest to your version. All 53 tests pass, and results
  match the original calculator bit for bit on 2,000,000 random deposits.

  private static boolean earnsInterest(PlanType planType, int days) {
      return days > planType.interestFreeDays()
              && days <= planType.lastInterestDay();
  }

  For this to work, PlanType now knows each plan's last interest day:

  BASIC("basic", 0.01, 30),
  STUDENT("student", 0.03, 30, 365),
  PREMIUM("premium", 0.05, 45);

  - New field and getter: lastInterestDay, with Javadoc on lastInterestDay().
  - Plans with no end date: a 3-argument constructor gives basic and premium
    Integer.MAX_VALUE. A whole-number days can never be larger, so for them the
    upper check is always true.
  - Calculator: STUDENT_LAST_INTEREST_DAY is gone, along with the special case
    for PlanType.STUDENT. The calculator no longer names any plan; every plan's
    terms are in the enum.

  I didn't add a PlanTypeTest case for lastInterestDay, because the
  characterization tests already check student days 365 and 366. The committed
  tests are untouched, and nothing is committed.

  TimeDeposit classes against the README requirements and SOLID principles.     
                                                                                
  We have decided NOT to support alternative day-count conventions such as      
  Actual/365. The existing monthly interest calculation must remain unchanged.

  principles in a pragmatic way.

  ⎿  Plan saved to: 
     ~/.claude/plans/pasted-content-id-9f1f-review-the-validated-iverson.md · 
     /plan to edit
     Design review: TimeDepositCalculator / PlanType / TimeDeposit

     Context

     Review of the refactored domain code (commit e87cd95 "clean code #1")
     against the README and SOLID, before the Spring/persistence work starts.
     Fixed by the author: the monthly calculation (balance * rate / 12), the
     rounding and all valid-input behavior stay unchanged. No other day-count
     conventions (such as Actual/365) will be supported.

     Findings

     1. Adding a new plan: one enum line. [verified by reading]
     - Any plan whose rule is "an annual rate plus an interest window
       (interestFreeDays, lastInterestDay]" is a single new constant in
       PlanType.
     - The calculator, TimeDeposit and the tests need no change, apart from new
       tests for the new plan.
     - Limit: a rule of a different shape (balance tiers, withdrawal-dependent
       rates) needs code. Add behavior to the enum at that point, not now.

     2. Plan data is encapsulated, but the eligibility rule is not.
     - Rate and window live in PlanType.
     - TimeDepositCalculator.earnsInterest(planType, days) reads two raw getters
       and applies the window rule itself (feature envy: one class doing work
       with another class's data).
     - Because of this, the Integer.MAX_VALUE meaning "no end date" leaks
       through the public lastInterestDay().

     3. The calculator has one clear job: apply one month of interest to each
     deposit. Its four steps are look up the plan, check eligibility, compute
     monthly interest, round and add. Converting the string to PlanType has to
     happen there, because TimeDeposit keeps String planType (shared API).

     4. SRP and Open/Closed are met pragmatically.
     - Responsibilities: TimeDeposit holds the data, PlanType the plan terms
       plus code lookup, TimeDepositCalculator the monthly update.
     - Open/Closed: new plans of the same shape don't touch the calculator.
     - No strategy or interface is justified: there is no variation in behavior,
       only in data.

     5. Further refactoring would add little. One small, worthwhile improvement
     remains (below). Everything else would be churn:
     - Strategy pattern or InterestPolicy interface: no concrete need.
     - OptionalInt instead of Integer.MAX_VALUE: more code, no gain once the
       value is private.
     - Moving balance * rate / 12 into the enum: "monthly" is the calculator's
       convention, and it stays there.
     - Replacing the fromCode stream: 3 values, no measurable cost.
     - Renaming the two overloaded monthlyInterest methods: marginal.

     6. The documentation is out of date. docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md still plans
     step 3 with InterestPolicy classes, "Policies are keyed by PlanType", and
     decision 3 ("30-day rule inside each policy"). The enum-based design has
     replaced all of that.

     Recommendation

     Keep the design as it is, with one small encapsulation fix and a doc sync:

     1. src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanType.java:
        - add public boolean earnsInterestOn(int days), returning days > 
          interestFreeDays && days <= lastInterestDay, with Javadoc;
        - remove the public interestFreeDays() and lastInterestDay() getters,
          which then have no callers, so the Integer.MAX_VALUE value stays
          private;
        - keep annualRate().
     2. src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculator.java: delete
        earnsInterest(...) and use .filter(planType -> 
        planType.earnsInterestOn(deposit.getDays())). Nothing else changes: the
        formula, its operation order and roundToTwoDecimals stay identical.
     3. docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md:
        - mark step 3 as "replaced by the PlanType enum (rate, interest-free
          days, last interest day)";
        - drop the InterestPolicy and Map<PlanType, InterestPolicy> text from §7
          and §8;
        - update decision 3: the interest window is defined per plan in
          PlanType.

     No test changes: the characterization tests already cover every window
     boundary (basic 29/30/31, student 30/31/365/366, premium 44/45/46).

     Verification

     - mvn test: 53 tests green; TimeDepositCalculatorTest unchanged (git diff 
       --quiet -- src/test).
     - Rerun the bit-for-bit comparison against the original calculator
       (scratchpad/equiv, 2,000,000 random cases): 0 differences.
     - grep -rn "interestFreeDays()\|lastInterestDay()\|InterestPolicy" src docs
       → no stale references.
     - Review the diff and do not commit.

⏺ I made the change from the approved plan. The review's verdict is that the
  design is sound as it is, apart from this one encapsulation fix, which is now
  in place.

  All 53 tests pass, with no test changes. Results still match the original
  calculator bit for bit on 2,000,000 random deposits. Nothing is committed.

  Review in short:
  - New plans: a plan of the same shape (a rate plus a range of days that earn
    interest) is one new line in PlanType.
  - Calculator: it has one clear job, applying one month of interest to each
    deposit.
  - SOLID: the single-responsibility and open/closed principles are met in a
    practical way.
  - No strategy classes or interfaces: the plans differ only in their data, not
    their behavior, so there's nothing for them to abstract.

  What changed:
  - PlanType: a new earnsInterestOn(int days) now holds the eligibility rule.
    The public getters interestFreeDays() and lastInterestDay() are gone, so the
    Integer.MAX_VALUE used for "no end date" is no longer visible outside the
    enum. annualRate() stays public.
  - TimeDepositCalculator: the private earnsInterest(...) is removed, and the
    calculator now calls .filter(planType -> 
    planType.earnsInterestOn(deposit.getDays())). The formula, its operation
    order and the rounding are unchanged.
  - docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md:
    - step 1 is marked done (a9f0265), step 2 done (e87cd95), and step 3 done
      but uncommitted;
    - step 3 now describes the PlanType enum instead of the planned
      InterestPolicy classes;
    - decision 3 and the architecture tree and notes match the enum design.

  The two places that still mention InterestPolicy in the doc are deliberate:
  they record why that design was dropped. Overall, 18 lines were added and 28
  removed across the three files.

❯ /plan                                                                         
  ⎿  Current plan
     /Users/cserhatit/.claude/plans/pasted-content-id-9f1f-review-the-validated-
     iverson.md

     Design review: TimeDepositCalculator / PlanType / TimeDeposit

     Context

     Review of the refactored domain code (commit e87cd95 "clean code #1")
     against the README and SOLID, before the Spring/persistence work starts.
     Fixed by the author: the monthly calculation (balance * rate / 12), the
     rounding and all valid-input behavior stay unchanged. No other day-count
     conventions (such as Actual/365) will be supported.

     Findings

     1. Adding a new plan: one enum line. [verified by reading]
     - Any plan whose rule is "an annual rate plus an interest window
       (interestFreeDays, lastInterestDay]" is a single new constant in
       PlanType.
     - The calculator, TimeDeposit and the tests need no change, apart from new
       tests for the new plan.
     - Limit: a rule of a different shape (balance tiers, withdrawal-dependent
       rates) needs code. Add behavior to the enum at that point, not now.

     2. Plan data is encapsulated, but the eligibility rule is not.
     - Rate and window live in PlanType.
     - TimeDepositCalculator.earnsInterest(planType, days) reads two raw getters
       and applies the window rule itself (feature envy: one class doing work
       with another class's data).
     - Because of this, the Integer.MAX_VALUE meaning "no end date" leaks
       through the public lastInterestDay().

     3. The calculator has one clear job: apply one month of interest to each
     deposit. Its four steps are look up the plan, check eligibility, compute
     monthly interest, round and add. Converting the string to PlanType has to
     happen there, because TimeDeposit keeps String planType (shared API).

     4. SRP and Open/Closed are met pragmatically.
     - Responsibilities: TimeDeposit holds the data, PlanType the plan terms
       plus code lookup, TimeDepositCalculator the monthly update.
     - Open/Closed: new plans of the same shape don't touch the calculator.
     - No strategy or interface is justified: there is no variation in behavior,
       only in data.

     5. Further refactoring would add little. One small, worthwhile improvement
     remains (below). Everything else would be churn:
     - Strategy pattern or InterestPolicy interface: no concrete need.
     - OptionalInt instead of Integer.MAX_VALUE: more code, no gain once the
       value is private.
     - Moving balance * rate / 12 into the enum: "monthly" is the calculator's
       convention, and it stays there.
     - Replacing the fromCode stream: 3 values, no measurable cost.
     - Renaming the two overloaded monthlyInterest methods: marginal.

     6. The documentation is out of date. docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md still plans
     step 3 with InterestPolicy classes, "Policies are keyed by PlanType", and
     decision 3 ("30-day rule inside each policy"). The enum-based design has
     replaced all of that.

     Recommendation

     Keep the design as it is, with one small encapsulation fix and a doc sync:

     1. src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanType.java:
        - add public boolean earnsInterestOn(int days), returning days > 
          interestFreeDays && days <= lastInterestDay, with Javadoc;
        - remove the public interestFreeDays() and lastInterestDay() getters,
          which then have no callers, so the Integer.MAX_VALUE value stays
          private;
        - keep annualRate().
     2. src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculator.java: delete
        earnsInterest(...) and use .filter(planType -> 
        planType.earnsInterestOn(deposit.getDays())). Nothing else changes: the
        formula, its operation order and roundToTwoDecimals stay identical.
     3. docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md:
        - mark step 3 as "replaced by the PlanType enum (rate, interest-free
          days, last interest day)";
        - drop the InterestPolicy and Map<PlanType, InterestPolicy> text from §7
          and §8;
        - update decision 3: the interest window is defined per plan in
          PlanType.

     No test changes: the characterization tests already cover every window
     boundary (basic 29/30/31, student 30/31/365/366, premium 44/45/46).

     Verification

     - mvn test: 53 tests green; TimeDepositCalculatorTest unchanged (git diff 
       --quiet -- src/test).
     - Rerun the bit-for-bit comparison against the original calculator
       (scratchpad/equiv, 2,000,000 random cases): 0 differences.
     - grep -rn "interestFreeDays()\|lastInterestDay()\|InterestPolicy" src docs
       → no stale references.
     - Review the diff and do not commit.

     "/plan open" to edit this plan in Vim
