  ⎿  Plan saved to: 
     ~/.claude/plans/pasted-content-id-9f1f-review-the-validated-iverson.md · 
     /plan to edit
     Architecture & implementation plan: Spring Boot, REST API, persistence 
     (rev. 3)

     Context

     The domain refactoring is committed (3302aca, docs 35762e0). The domain
     consists of:
     - TimeDeposit (shared, String planType, Double balance);
     - PlanType (enum: each plan's code, rate and interest window);
     - TimeDepositCalculator.updateBalance(List<TimeDeposit>).

     It is covered by 43 characterization tests plus 10 PlanTypeTest tests. Next
     comes what the README asks for: two REST endpoints and a PostgreSQL
     database with timeDeposits and withdrawals, using a pragmatic hexagonal
     structure. The domain code and the existing tests stay unchanged.

     Revision history:
     - rev. 2: bootstrap order, query count, separate demo data, money boundary,
       focused tests.
     - rev. 3 (author decisions):
       - short-lived transactions;
       - no application-level transaction for GET;
       - one short @Transactional for POST;
       - no locking (concurrency is outside the README);
       - open-in-view disabled.

     Repository facts that shape the plan:
     - plain Maven, no wrapper;
     - Java 17 target; the JDK is 21;
     - Docker is installed but not running; it must be started before step 2;
     - latest Spring Boot 3.x on Maven Central: 3.5.16 (checked).

     ---

     1. Requirements: explicit vs. assumed

     Explicit (README):
     - Exactly two endpoints:
       - (a) update the balances of all deposits;
       - (b) get all deposits as id, planType, balance, days, withdrawals.
     - Tables:
       - timeDeposits(id int PK, planType string req, days int req, balance 
         decimal req);
       - withdrawals(id int PK, timeDepositId int FK req, amount decimal req, 
         date date req).
     - Keep the TimeDeposit API and the updateBalance signature and behavior.
     - Use a Swagger/OpenAPI contract, with instructions for calling the
       endpoints through Swagger.
     - Invalid input and exceptions need not be handled.

     Assumed: everything in §9.

     2. REST contract

     ┌────────────────────────┬─────────┬─────────┬────────────────────────┐
     │        Endpoint        │ Request │ Success │          Body          │
     ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────┤
     │ POST /time-deposits/ba │ none    │ 204 No  │ none                   │
     │ lance-updates          │         │ Content │                        │
     ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────┤
     │ GET /time-deposits     │ none    │ 200 OK  │ TimeDepositResponse[], │
     │                        │         │         │  ordered by id         │
     └────────────────────────┴─────────┴─────────┴────────────────────────┘

     - Response DTOs (records in the web adapter):
       - TimeDepositResponse(int id, String planType, BigDecimal balance, int 
         days, List<WithdrawalResponse> withdrawals);
       - WithdrawalResponse(int id, BigDecimal amount, LocalDate date).
     - JSON values: money is a number with two decimals (10008.33, 10000.00);
       dates are ISO strings ("2026-01-15").
     - Errors: Spring's default 500, with no custom error handling (README).
       There are no request DTOs, because neither endpoint takes input.
     - Swagger: UI at /swagger-ui.html, JSON at /v3/api-docs, generated
       code-first from @Operation / @ApiResponse annotations.

     3. Responsibilities

     Part: Domain (unchanged: TimeDeposit, PlanType, TimeDepositCalculator;
       new: Withdrawal record with BigDecimal amount and LocalDate date)
     Responsibility: Interest rules and domain values. No framework
       dependencies.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Application: TimeDepositService
     Responsibility: updateAllBalances(): the only application-level
       transaction (§5.5). getAllTimeDeposits(): delegates to the port, with
       no @Transactional.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Output port: TimeDepositRepository (interface in the application
       package)
     Responsibility: The storage operations the application needs. The only
       interface in the design: it keeps the application and domain
       independent of JPA.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Input adapter: TimeDepositController + response records
     Responsibility: HTTP ↔ use case; domain → DTO mapping; OpenAPI
       annotations.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Persistence adapter: entities, TimeDepositJpaRepository,
       TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter
     Responsibility: JPA mapping; loads and maps everything a read needs
       before returning, so no entity leaves the adapter; the money boundary
       (§5.4).

     Deliberately left out: input-port interfaces, one interface per use case,
     mapper frameworks, and request DTOs.

     4. Packages and dependency direction

     org.ikigaidigital                         ← domain (shared FQNs, fixed) + 
     TimeDepositApplication (@SpringBootApplication, @Bean 
     TimeDepositCalculator)
     org.ikigaidigital.application             ← TimeDepositService, 
     TimeDepositRepository (port), TimeDepositWithWithdrawals (read record)
     org.ikigaidigital.adapter.in.web          ← TimeDepositController, 
     TimeDepositResponse, WithdrawalResponse
     org.ikigaidigital.adapter.out.persistence ← TimeDepositEntity, 
     WithdrawalEntity, TimeDepositJpaRepository, TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter
     - Direction: adapter.* → application → domain.
     - Calculator bean: TimeDepositCalculator stays free of annotations; the
       main class declares it as a @Bean.

     5. Persistence

     5.1 Schema migrations and demo data, kept separate

     - Schema: src/main/resources/db/migration/V1__create_tables.sql, applied in
       every environment.
     CREATE TABLE "timeDeposits" (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, "planType" VARCHAR(50)
     NOT NULL,
                                  days INTEGER NOT NULL, balance NUMERIC(19,2)
     NOT NULL);
     CREATE TABLE withdrawals (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
                               "timeDepositId" INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES
     "timeDeposits"(id),
                               amount NUMERIC(19,2) NOT NULL, date DATE NOT 
     NULL);
     CREATE INDEX withdrawals_time_deposit_id_idx ON
     withdrawals("timeDepositId");
       - No CHECK constraint on planType: unknown plans stay storable and
         earn 0.
       - No ID generation: there is no create endpoint.
     - Demo data: src/main/resources/db/demo/R__demo_data.sql.
       - A Flyway repeatable migration with INSERT … ON CONFLICT (id) DO 
         NOTHING, so it is safe to re-run.
       - Enabled only by the dev profile: application-dev.properties sets
         spring.flyway.locations=classpath:db/migration,classpath:db/demo.
       - The default configuration applies only db/migration. Integration tests
         use their own @Sql fixtures.
       - Trade-off: changing the script doesn't update rows that already exist.
         That's acceptable for demo data; reset with docker compose down -v.

     5.2 JPA mapping and configuration

     - Naming: spring.jpa.hibernate.naming.physical-strategy=org.hibernate.boot.
       model.naming.PhysicalNamingStrategyStandardImpl plus
       spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.globally_quoted_identifiers=true.
       - The README's camelCase names then map exactly through @Table(name = 
         "timeDeposits") and @Column(name = "planType").
     - Schema ownership: ddl-auto=validate, so Flyway owns the schema.
     - No Open Session in View: spring.jpa.open-in-view=false. This guarantees
       the database session never stays open during JSON rendering; anything a
       response needs must be loaded inside the adapter.
     - Batching: hibernate.jdbc.batch_size=50 and hibernate.order_updates=true
       send balance updates in batches.
     - Entities:
       - TimeDepositEntity has a lazy @OneToMany(mappedBy = "timeDeposit") 
         List<WithdrawalEntity>;
       - WithdrawalEntity has a @ManyToOne back;
       - money fields are BigDecimal.

     5.3 Port methods and SQL per request

     Port method: List<TimeDeposit> findAll() (update path)
     Adapter implementation: @Query("select d from TimeDepositEntity d order
       by d.id") → map to TimeDeposit; withdrawals are not touched, so not
       loaded
     SQL: 1 × SELECT
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Port method: void saveBalances(List<TimeDeposit>)
     Adapter implementation: getReferenceById(id).setBalance(toStoredBalance(…))
       for each deposit; Hibernate writes on commit
     SQL: 0 × SELECT; ≤ N × UPDATE (only rows that changed), batched
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Port method: List<TimeDepositWithWithdrawals> findAllWithWithdrawals()
       (GET)
     Adapter implementation: select d from TimeDepositEntity d left join
       fetch d.withdrawals order by d.id; mapped to domain records inside
       the adapter method
     SQL: 1 × SELECT (no N+1)

     - Why getReferenceById costs no query: it returns the instance findAll()
       already loaded, because both run inside the POST's single transaction. A
       code comment states this contract. Outside that transaction it would
       still be correct, just slower.
     - GET without an application transaction:
       - The fetch join loads every deposit and withdrawal in one query, and the
         adapter maps to domain records before returning, so nothing lazy is
         touched after the database session closes.
       - The query runs in the transaction Spring Data opens for the repository
         call itself, or in a short-lived session; it is not held across the
         service or controller.
       - Consistency is fine: one SQL statement sees one consistent database
         state, so no transaction spanning several queries is needed.
       - Because open-in-view is off, any missing fetch fails loudly
         (LazyInitializationException) in the API test instead of quietly
         running extra queries.

     5.4 Money boundary (BigDecimal ↔ double)

     - Loading: entity.getBalance().doubleValue(). A 2-decimal value maps to the
       nearest double and back without loss up to about 9×10¹³; beyond that,
       the legacy double precision applies.
     - Saving: toStoredBalance(double) = BigDecimal.valueOf(value).setScale(2, 
       RoundingMode.HALF_UP). This is explicit; we don't leave it to the
       database's implicit rounding.
     - The stored value can differ from the calculator's raw double:
       - student 1234567.89 → the calculator gives 1237654.3099999998 (pinned by
         the characterization test);
       - stored and returned value: 1237654.31;
       - the next POST calculates from 1237654.31.
     - Consequence: applying N months through the API can, in rare cases, differ
       by a cent from N in-memory calls. The calculator is unchanged; the
       README's Decimal column imposes the two decimals.
     - JSON: BigDecimal.valueOf(double).setScale(2, HALF_UP); every value has
       two decimals.
     - Where it's documented: README, INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md and a comment on
       toStoredBalance.
     - Where it's tested: persistence integration test and API test (§7).

     5.5 Transactions and concurrency

     - POST: one short @Transactional on TimeDepositService.updateAllBalances()
       covering findAll() → calculator.updateBalance(deposits) →
       saveBalances(deposits).
       - It contains no remote calls and no waiting on input: one SELECT,
         in-memory calculation, batched UPDATEs, commit.
       - It is atomic: either every balance is updated or none is.
     - GET: no @Transactional in the application layer (§5.3).
     - No locking, neither optimistic nor pessimistic. Concurrency control is
       outside the README requirements. The known risk is documented, not
       solved:
       - Lost update: PostgreSQL's default isolation is READ COMMITTED. Two
         POSTs running at the same time can both read the same balances; each
         then writes an absolute value, old + interest. The second UPDATE waits
         for the first commit and then overwrites it: both requests return 204,
         but only one month is applied.
       - Possible later fixes (not implemented): SELECT … FOR UPDATE ORDER BY id
         (one @Lock annotation, no deadlocks thanks to the fixed ordering), or
         optimistic @Version (needs a new column and retry logic).
     - Why the POST is not split into a read outside the transaction and a write
       inside it:
       - Stale read: balances read outside the transaction can be overwritten by
         another writer before the write transaction starts. The calculation
         then uses outdated values, and the write silently replaces newer data.
         This widens the lost-update window from milliseconds to the whole
         request.
       - Lost atomicity: the read and the write would no longer be one unit. A
         failure between them, or a concurrent change, leaves no guarantee that
         the stored balances come from one consistent snapshot.
       - No real gain: the read is a single fast query, so moving it out doesn't
         shorten the transaction in any meaningful way.
       - Therefore load, calculation and save stay inside one short transaction.
     - Concurrency safety is not idempotency (documented separately):
       - Concurrency safety means POSTs running at the same time don't lose each
         other's updates. It is not provided now (see the lost-update risk
         above).
       - Idempotency means a repeated or retried POST has the same effect as a
         single call. It is not provided either, by design: every POST applies
         another month (legacy calculator behavior). Preventing it would need a
         "last applied" marker or a request key, which aren't in the README.
       - Locking alone would fix concurrency safety but not idempotency.

     6. Reusing TimeDepositCalculator unchanged

     - The service calls calculator.updateBalance(deposits) exactly as it is.
       The adapter builds deposits with new TimeDeposit(id, planType, 
       balance.doubleValue(), days) and reads them back with getBalance().
     - BigDecimal exists only at the database and JSON boundaries (§5.4).
     - None of TimeDeposit, PlanType, TimeDepositCalculator or the existing
       tests change. Every step checks this with git diff.

     7. Test suite

     A focused set: no Mockito, no service unit test, no @WebMvcTest. Those
     would overlap with the real-stack API test.

     Test: TimeDepositCalculatorTest, PlanTypeTest
     Type: Unit, unchanged
     Proves: Domain behavior
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Test: TimeDepositPersistenceAdapterTest
     Type: @DataJpaTest + @AutoConfigureTestDatabase(replace = NONE) +
       Testcontainers PostgreSQL + Flyway;
       @Import(TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter)
     Proves: The schema migrates and the quoted camelCase mapping validates;
       findAllWithWithdrawals returns deposits with their withdrawals ([]
       when there are none) ordered by id; the money boundary: saving
       1237654.3099999998 stores 1237654.31, and a NUMERIC value loads as
       the expected double
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Test: DemoDataMigrationTest
     Type: @DataJpaTest with db/migration + db/demo
     Proves: The demo script applies and loads its rows
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Test: TimeDepositApiTest
     Type: @SpringBootTest(RANDOM_PORT) + Testcontainers + @Sql fixture;
       default profile, open-in-view off
     Proves: POST → 204; GET → 200 with exact JSON: field names, withdrawals
       shape, balances 10008.33 / 10041.67 / unchanged for student day 366,
       premium day 45 and an unknown plan; the money boundary through the
       API (student 1234567.89 → 1237654.31); a second POST compounds
       (10016.67); GET works without an application transaction (no
       LazyInitializationException); /v3/api-docs documents exactly the two
       API paths

     - Shared container: one TestcontainersConfiguration with a single shared
       PostgreSQLContainer and @ServiceConnection.
     - No concurrency test: there's no locking, and the lost-update risk is
       documented rather than tested.
     - Trade-off: the API test is slower and points less precisely at the
       failing part than slice tests, but at this size real-stack coverage is
       worth more than mocked overlap.

     8. Implementation sequence

     Each step is one commit, and mvn test stays green after each.

     1. Boot skeleton, no database yet.
        - pom.xml: parent spring-boot-starter-parent:3.5.16,
          spring-boot-starter-web, springdoc-openapi-starter-webmvc-ui (a 2.x
          version compatible with Boot 3.5; confirmed by the step-4 api-docs
          test), spring-boot-starter-test.
        - Drop the explicit JUnit, AssertJ and surefire versions; the Boot BOM
          manages them.
        - Add TimeDepositApplication and TimeDepositCalculator as a @Bean.
        - No JPA, Flyway or driver dependencies yet, which would trigger
          DataSource auto-configuration and fail without a database. No new 
          test.
        - Check: the 53 existing tests pass under the managed versions.
     2. Database and Testcontainers together.
        - Add spring-boot-starter-data-jpa, flyway-core,
          flyway-database-postgresql, postgresql (runtime);
          spring-boot-testcontainers and org.testcontainers:postgresql (test).
        - Add V1 schema, application.properties (datasource URL from environment
          variables, defaulting to the compose database; JPA settings from
          §5.2, including open-in-view=false), application-dev.properties,
          R__demo_data.sql, compose.yaml and TestcontainersConfiguration.
        - Check: DemoDataMigrationTest, the first test that needs PostgreSQL,
          added together with the Testcontainers setup.
     3. Persistence: domain Withdrawal; entities, TimeDepositJpaRepository,
        port, read record, adapter.
        - Check: TimeDepositPersistenceAdapterTest.
     4. Application and web: TimeDepositService (@Transactional on
        updateAllBalances only), controller, response records, OpenAPI
        annotations.
        - Check: TimeDepositApiTest.
     5. Documentation:
        - README: run with docker compose up -d and mvn spring-boot:run 
          -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=dev, Swagger "Try it out" steps,
          assumptions, the money boundary, the transaction design, the
          lost-update risk vs. idempotency;
        - update INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md and AI_USAGE.md;
        - dependency and dead-code review.

     9. Ambiguities to document

     1. Endpoints: POST /time-deposits/balance-updates → 204 and GET 
        /time-deposits → 200. The README specifies neither verbs nor paths.
     2. "Exactly two endpoints": springdoc's /v3/api-docs and /swagger-ui are
        documentation, required by the README's Swagger requirement, not API
        endpoints.
     3. Idempotency: each POST applies another month, and days is unchanged. Not
        idempotent, by design (legacy).
     4. Concurrency: no locking. Two POSTs running at the same time can lose one
        month (lost update). A known limitation that is outside the README;
        fixes are listed in §5.5.
     5. Money: NUMERIC(19,2); the stored value is rounded HALF_UP to two
        decimals and can differ from the calculator's raw double (§5.4).
     6. Names: the README's camelCase table and column names are kept exactly,
        using quoted identifiers.
     7. Plan types: not constrained in the database; unknown types are stored
        and earn 0.
     8. withdrawals: an array of {id, amount, date} ordered by id; [] when there
        are none; withdrawals don't affect interest.
     9. Order: deposits are returned ordered by id.
     10. IDs: plain INTEGER primary keys with no generation, because no endpoint
         creates records.
     11. Demo data: loaded only with the dev profile; never in the default
         configuration or in tests.
     12. days: out of scope. It is stored data, and nothing increments it.
     13. Dates: LocalDate with no time zone, as ISO strings.

     Verification

     - After every step:
       - mvn test is green;
       - git diff --quiet <step-base> -- 
         src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDeposit.java 
         src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculator.java 
         src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanType.java 
         src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculatorTest.java 
         src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanTypeTest.java shows nothing
         changed.
     - At the end: docker compose up -d && mvn spring-boot:run 
       -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=dev, then in Swagger UI call GET → POST → GET
       and check the balances against the demo rows.
     - Without the dev profile: GET returns [].
     - Prerequisite: start Docker before step 2.

※ recap: We're adding Spring Boot, the two REST endpoints and PostgreSQL to the time deposit kata; steps 1–2 (skeleton, schema, Testcontainers) are done, uncommitted, with all 54 tests passing. Next is your review and commit, then I'll build the persistence adapter in step 3.

⏺ Step 3: the persistence adapter. One correction to the plan while
  implementing: saveBalances gets @Transactional on the adapter, with Spring's
  default propagation (REQUIRED), so it joins the caller's transaction. Inside
  the POST transaction that changes nothing. Without it, though, calling
  getReferenceById outside a transaction returns an uninitialized proxy, and
  setting the balance would throw. The plan's claim that it would be "still
  correct, just slower" was wrong.

⏺ Step 3 (the persistence adapter) is done and all 57 tests pass. I haven't
  committed anything.

  New code:
  - Domain: a Withdrawal record (id, BigDecimal amount, LocalDate date), with no
    framework dependencies.
  - Application:
    - TimeDepositRepository, the interface the application uses for storage,
      with findAll(), saveBalances(...) and findAllWithWithdrawals();
    - TimeDepositWithWithdrawals, a record pairing a deposit with its
      withdrawals.
  - Persistence (all classes package-private, so nothing outside this package
    sees the JPA entities):
    - TimeDepositEntity and WithdrawalEntity, mapping the README's camelCase
      tables. Thanks to the naming settings from step 2, only @Table and
      @JoinColumn needed explicit names. Withdrawals are sorted by
      @OrderBy("id").
    - TimeDepositJpaRepository: findAllByOrderByIdAsc(), plus one fetch-join
      query that loads deposits and their withdrawals together.
    - TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter: maps entities to domain objects. Its
      toStoredBalance converts the double balance back to two decimals, with a
      comment explaining why the stored value can differ.

  Change from the approved plan: saveBalances is annotated @Transactional. With
  Spring's default propagation it simply joins the POST transaction, so the
  plan's behavior is unchanged. Without it, calling getReferenceById outside a
  transaction would hand back an uninitialized proxy and the update would throw.
  The plan's claim that it would be "still correct, just slower" was wrong.

  TimeDepositPersistenceAdapterTest (PostgreSQL via Testcontainers, fixture 
  src/test/resources/time-deposits.sql):
  - findAll maps every column and returns deposits ordered by id.
  - findAllWithWithdrawals returns withdrawals ordered by id (the fixture
    inserts them out of order) and an empty list for a deposit without any.
  - Money boundary: saving the calculator's raw 1237654.3099999998 stores
    1237654.31 and reads back as 1237654.31. Balances that didn't change stay
    the same.

  Query counts, checked in Hibernate's SQL log:
  - Update path: 1 SELECT … ORDER BY id, then no extra SELECT, and 1 UPDATE for
    the single changed row. The unchanged deposit wasn't written at all.
  - Read path: 1 SELECT that loads deposits and withdrawals together, so the
    number of queries doesn't grow with the number of deposits.

  Unchanged: the domain classes and the existing tests (git diff).

  Next: step 4 adds TimeDepositService, the controller with its response records
  and Swagger annotations, and TimeDepositApiTest.

⏺ Step 4 (the application service, REST endpoints and Swagger annotations) is
  done. All 61 tests pass, and a manual run of the real app worked end to end. I
  haven't committed anything.

  New code:
  - TimeDepositService:
    - updateAllBalances() runs in one short @Transactional: load all deposits →
      calculator.updateBalance(...) unchanged → save the balances.
    - getAllTimeDeposits() has no @Transactional; the persistence adapter loads
      everything in one query.
    - Its Javadoc states that the update isn't idempotent and that two
      concurrent updates can lose one month.
  - TimeDepositController:
    - POST /time-deposits/balance-updates → 204;
    - GET /time-deposits → 200;
    - Swagger documentation via @Tag, @Operation and @ApiResponse.
  - TimeDepositResponse / WithdrawalResponse: response records with Swagger
    examples. Balances are returned as BigDecimal with two decimals.

  TimeDepositApiTest (real HTTP, PostgreSQL, no session open during JSON 
  rendering):
  - GET: the whole JSON response matches exactly, including withdrawals in id
    order, [] when there are none, and 10000.00 rather than 10000.0. This also
    shows GET works with no application transaction.
  - POST: 204, then the balances are 10008.33, 10041.67 and 1237654.31 (where
    the calculator's raw value is 1237654.3099999998). Student after day 365,
    premium within 45 days and the unknown plan stay unchanged.
  - Two POSTs: the second applies another month, giving 10016.67.
  - /v3/api-docs: documents exactly the two paths, with exactly get and post.

  Test fix: DemoDataMigrationTest failed in the full run. The API test commits
  its fixture rows to the shared test database, and when the demo migration then
  ran there, ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING skipped its own rows. The real problem is
  that tests with different Flyway settings shared one database, so results
  depended on test order. The demo test now has its own PostgreSQL container,
  which costs about 1 second. Its Javadoc explains why.

  Manual end-to-end (docker compose up, the dev profile, curl):
  - GET returned 6 demo deposits with their withdrawals.
  - POST → 204.
  - GET returned basic 10000 → 10008.33, student 20000 → 20050.00, premium
    100000 → 100416.67. The basic deposit still in its first 30 days, the
    student past 365 days and the premium within 45 days were unchanged.
  - Swagger UI returned 200. I ran docker compose down -v afterwards.

  Unchanged: the domain classes and the existing tests (git diff).

  assumptions, the money boundary, the transaction and concurrency notes, plus

  review.

    covers (Docker required), and the package structure.
  - Design decisions and assumptions:
    - endpoint verbs and paths;
    - interest rules;
    - each POST applies another month (not idempotent);
    - transaction design;
    - concurrency not handled: two simultaneous POSTs can lose one month.
      Locking would fix that but still wouldn't make the POST idempotent;
    - the money boundary (1237654.3099999998 → 1237654.31);
    - the camelCase table names, planType, IDs and withdrawals.

❯ /plan                                                                         
  ⎿  Current plan
     /Users/cserhatit/.claude/plans/pasted-content-id-9f1f-review-the-validated-
     iverson.md

     Architecture & implementation plan: Spring Boot, REST API, persistence 
     (rev. 3)

     Context

     The domain refactoring is committed (3302aca, docs 35762e0). The domain
     consists of:
     - TimeDeposit (shared, String planType, Double balance);
     - PlanType (enum: each plan's code, rate and interest window);
     - TimeDepositCalculator.updateBalance(List<TimeDeposit>).

     It is covered by 43 characterization tests plus 10 PlanTypeTest tests. Next
     comes what the README asks for: two REST endpoints and a PostgreSQL
     database with timeDeposits and withdrawals, using a pragmatic hexagonal
     structure. The domain code and the existing tests stay unchanged.

     Revision history:
     - rev. 2: bootstrap order, query count, separate demo data, money boundary,
       focused tests.
     - rev. 3 (author decisions):
       - short-lived transactions;
       - no application-level transaction for GET;
       - one short @Transactional for POST;
       - no locking (concurrency is outside the README);
       - open-in-view disabled.

     Repository facts that shape the plan:
     - plain Maven, no wrapper;
     - Java 17 target; the JDK is 21;
     - Docker is installed but not running; it must be started before step 2;
     - latest Spring Boot 3.x on Maven Central: 3.5.16 (checked).

     ---

     1. Requirements: explicit vs. assumed

     Explicit (README):
     - Exactly two endpoints:
       - (a) update the balances of all deposits;
       - (b) get all deposits as id, planType, balance, days, withdrawals.
     - Tables:
       - timeDeposits(id int PK, planType string req, days int req, balance 
         decimal req);
       - withdrawals(id int PK, timeDepositId int FK req, amount decimal req, 
         date date req).
     - Keep the TimeDeposit API and the updateBalance signature and behavior.
     - Use a Swagger/OpenAPI contract, with instructions for calling the
       endpoints through Swagger.
     - Invalid input and exceptions need not be handled.

     Assumed: everything in §9.

     2. REST contract

     ┌─────────────────────────────┬─────────┬─────────┬────────────────────────
     ┐
     │          Endpoint           │ Request │ Success │          Body
     │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────
     ┤
     │ POST /time-deposits/balance │ none    │ 204 No  │ none
     │
     │ -updates                    │         │ Content │
     │
     ├─────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────────
     ┤
     │ GET /time-deposits          │ none    │ 200 OK  │ TimeDepositResponse[],
     │
     │                             │         │         │  ordered by id
     │
     └─────────────────────────────┴─────────┴─────────┴────────────────────────
     ┘

     - Response DTOs (records in the web adapter):
       - TimeDepositResponse(int id, String planType, BigDecimal balance, int 
         days, List<WithdrawalResponse> withdrawals);
       - WithdrawalResponse(int id, BigDecimal amount, LocalDate date).
     - JSON values: money is a number with two decimals (10008.33, 10000.00);
       dates are ISO strings ("2026-01-15").
     - Errors: Spring's default 500, with no custom error handling (README).
       There are no request DTOs, because neither endpoint takes input.
     - Swagger: UI at /swagger-ui.html, JSON at /v3/api-docs, generated
       code-first from @Operation / @ApiResponse annotations.

     3. Responsibilities

     Part: Domain (unchanged: TimeDeposit, PlanType, TimeDepositCalculator; new:
       Withdrawal record with BigDecimal amount and LocalDate date)
     Responsibility: Interest rules and domain values. No framework
     dependencies.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Application: TimeDepositService
     Responsibility: updateAllBalances(): the only application-level transaction
       (§5.5). getAllTimeDeposits(): delegates to the port, with no
       @Transactional.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Output port: TimeDepositRepository (interface in the application
       package)
     Responsibility: The storage operations the application needs. The only
       interface in the design: it keeps the application and domain independent
       of JPA.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Input adapter: TimeDepositController + response records
     Responsibility: HTTP ↔ use case; domain → DTO mapping; OpenAPI annotations.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Part: Persistence adapter: entities, TimeDepositJpaRepository,
       TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter
     Responsibility: JPA mapping; loads and maps everything a read needs before
       returning, so no entity leaves the adapter; the money boundary (§5.4).

     Deliberately left out: input-port interfaces, one interface per use case,
     mapper frameworks, and request DTOs.

     4. Packages and dependency direction

     org.ikigaidigital                         ← domain (shared FQNs, fixed) + 
     TimeDepositApplication (@SpringBootApplication, @Bean 
     TimeDepositCalculator)
     org.ikigaidigital.application             ← TimeDepositService, 
     TimeDepositRepository (port), TimeDepositWithWithdrawals (read record)
     org.ikigaidigital.adapter.in.web          ← TimeDepositController, 
     TimeDepositResponse, WithdrawalResponse
     org.ikigaidigital.adapter.out.persistence ← TimeDepositEntity, 
     WithdrawalEntity, TimeDepositJpaRepository, TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter
     - Direction: adapter.* → application → domain.
     - Calculator bean: TimeDepositCalculator stays free of annotations; the
       main class declares it as a @Bean.

     5. Persistence

     5.1 Schema migrations and demo data, kept separate

     - Schema: src/main/resources/db/migration/V1__create_tables.sql, applied in
       every environment.
     CREATE TABLE "timeDeposits" (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, "planType" VARCHAR(50)
     NOT NULL,
                                  days INTEGER NOT NULL, balance NUMERIC(19,2)
     NOT NULL);
     CREATE TABLE withdrawals (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
                               "timeDepositId" INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES
     "timeDeposits"(id),
                               amount NUMERIC(19,2) NOT NULL, date DATE NOT 
     NULL);
     CREATE INDEX withdrawals_time_deposit_id_idx ON
     withdrawals("timeDepositId");
       - No CHECK constraint on planType: unknown plans stay storable and
         earn 0.
       - No ID generation: there is no create endpoint.
     - Demo data: src/main/resources/db/demo/R__demo_data.sql.
       - A Flyway repeatable migration with INSERT … ON CONFLICT (id) DO 
         NOTHING, so it is safe to re-run.
       - Enabled only by the dev profile: application-dev.properties sets
         spring.flyway.locations=classpath:db/migration,classpath:db/demo.
       - The default configuration applies only db/migration. Integration tests
         use their own @Sql fixtures.
       - Trade-off: changing the script doesn't update rows that already exist.
         That's acceptable for demo data; reset with docker compose down -v.

     5.2 JPA mapping and configuration

     - Naming: spring.jpa.hibernate.naming.physical-strategy=org.hibernate.boot.
       model.naming.PhysicalNamingStrategyStandardImpl plus
       spring.jpa.properties.hibernate.globally_quoted_identifiers=true.
       - The README's camelCase names then map exactly through @Table(name = 
         "timeDeposits") and @Column(name = "planType").
     - Schema ownership: ddl-auto=validate, so Flyway owns the schema.
     - No Open Session in View: spring.jpa.open-in-view=false. This guarantees
       the database session never stays open during JSON rendering; anything a
       response needs must be loaded inside the adapter.
     - Batching: hibernate.jdbc.batch_size=50 and hibernate.order_updates=true
       send balance updates in batches.
     - Entities:
       - TimeDepositEntity has a lazy @OneToMany(mappedBy = "timeDeposit") 
         List<WithdrawalEntity>;
       - WithdrawalEntity has a @ManyToOne back;
       - money fields are BigDecimal.

     5.3 Port methods and SQL per request

     Port method: List<TimeDeposit> findAll() (update path)
     Adapter implementation: @Query("select d from TimeDepositEntity d order by
       d.id") → map to TimeDeposit; withdrawals are not touched, so not loaded
     SQL: 1 × SELECT
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Port method: void saveBalances(List<TimeDeposit>)
     Adapter implementation: getReferenceById(id).setBalance(toStoredBalance(…))
       for each deposit; Hibernate writes on commit
     SQL: 0 × SELECT; ≤ N × UPDATE (only rows that changed), batched
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Port method: List<TimeDepositWithWithdrawals> findAllWithWithdrawals()
     (GET)
     Adapter implementation: select d from TimeDepositEntity d left join fetch
       d.withdrawals order by d.id; mapped to domain records inside the adapter
       method
     SQL: 1 × SELECT (no N+1)

     - Why getReferenceById costs no query: it returns the instance findAll()
       already loaded, because both run inside the POST's single transaction. A
       code comment states this contract. Outside that transaction it would
       still be correct, just slower.
     - GET without an application transaction:
       - The fetch join loads every deposit and withdrawal in one query, and the
         adapter maps to domain records before returning, so nothing lazy is
         touched after the database session closes.
       - The query runs in the transaction Spring Data opens for the repository
         call itself, or in a short-lived session; it is not held across the
         service or controller.
       - Consistency is fine: one SQL statement sees one consistent database
         state, so no transaction spanning several queries is needed.
       - Because open-in-view is off, any missing fetch fails loudly
         (LazyInitializationException) in the API test instead of quietly
         running extra queries.

     5.4 Money boundary (BigDecimal ↔ double)

     - Loading: entity.getBalance().doubleValue(). A 2-decimal value maps to the
       nearest double and back without loss up to about 9×10¹³; beyond that,
       the legacy double precision applies.
     - Saving: toStoredBalance(double) = BigDecimal.valueOf(value).setScale(2, 
       RoundingMode.HALF_UP). This is explicit; we don't leave it to the
       database's implicit rounding.
     - The stored value can differ from the calculator's raw double:
       - student 1234567.89 → the calculator gives 1237654.3099999998 (pinned by
         the characterization test);
       - stored and returned value: 1237654.31;
       - the next POST calculates from 1237654.31.
     - Consequence: applying N months through the API can, in rare cases, differ
       by a cent from N in-memory calls. The calculator is unchanged; the
       README's Decimal column imposes the two decimals.
     - JSON: BigDecimal.valueOf(double).setScale(2, HALF_UP); every value has
       two decimals.
     - Where it's documented: README, INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md and a comment on
       toStoredBalance.
     - Where it's tested: persistence integration test and API test (§7).

     5.5 Transactions and concurrency

     - POST: one short @Transactional on TimeDepositService.updateAllBalances()
       covering findAll() → calculator.updateBalance(deposits) →
       saveBalances(deposits).
       - It contains no remote calls and no waiting on input: one SELECT,
         in-memory calculation, batched UPDATEs, commit.
       - It is atomic: either every balance is updated or none is.
     - GET: no @Transactional in the application layer (§5.3).
     - No locking, neither optimistic nor pessimistic. Concurrency control is
       outside the README requirements. The known risk is documented, not
       solved:
       - Lost update: PostgreSQL's default isolation is READ COMMITTED. Two
         POSTs running at the same time can both read the same balances; each
         then writes an absolute value, old + interest. The second UPDATE waits
         for the first commit and then overwrites it: both requests return 204,
         but only one month is applied.
       - Possible later fixes (not implemented): SELECT … FOR UPDATE ORDER BY id
         (one @Lock annotation, no deadlocks thanks to the fixed ordering), or
         optimistic @Version (needs a new column and retry logic).
     - Why the POST is not split into a read outside the transaction and a write
       inside it:
       - Stale read: balances read outside the transaction can be overwritten by
         another writer before the write transaction starts. The calculation
         then uses outdated values, and the write silently replaces newer data.
         This widens the lost-update window from milliseconds to the whole
         request.
       - Lost atomicity: the read and the write would no longer be one unit. A
         failure between them, or a concurrent change, leaves no guarantee that
         the stored balances come from one consistent snapshot.
       - No real gain: the read is a single fast query, so moving it out doesn't
         shorten the transaction in any meaningful way.
       - Therefore load, calculation and save stay inside one short transaction.
     - Concurrency safety is not idempotency (documented separately):
       - Concurrency safety means POSTs running at the same time don't lose each
         other's updates. It is not provided now (see the lost-update risk
         above).
       - Idempotency means a repeated or retried POST has the same effect as a
         single call. It is not provided either, by design: every POST applies
         another month (legacy calculator behavior). Preventing it would need a
         "last applied" marker or a request key, which aren't in the README.
       - Locking alone would fix concurrency safety but not idempotency.

     6. Reusing TimeDepositCalculator unchanged

     - The service calls calculator.updateBalance(deposits) exactly as it is.
       The adapter builds deposits with new TimeDeposit(id, planType, 
       balance.doubleValue(), days) and reads them back with getBalance().
     - BigDecimal exists only at the database and JSON boundaries (§5.4).
     - None of TimeDeposit, PlanType, TimeDepositCalculator or the existing
       tests change. Every step checks this with git diff.

     7. Test suite

     A focused set: no Mockito, no service unit test, no @WebMvcTest. Those
     would overlap with the real-stack API test.

     Test: TimeDepositCalculatorTest, PlanTypeTest
     Type: Unit, unchanged
     Proves: Domain behavior
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Test: TimeDepositPersistenceAdapterTest
     Type: @DataJpaTest + @AutoConfigureTestDatabase(replace = NONE) +
       Testcontainers PostgreSQL + Flyway;
     @Import(TimeDepositPersistenceAdapter)
     Proves: The schema migrates and the quoted camelCase mapping validates;
       findAllWithWithdrawals returns deposits with their withdrawals ([] when
       there are none) ordered by id; the money boundary: saving
       1237654.3099999998 stores 1237654.31, and a NUMERIC value loads as the
       expected double
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Test: DemoDataMigrationTest
     Type: @DataJpaTest with db/migration + db/demo
     Proves: The demo script applies and loads its rows
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Test: TimeDepositApiTest
     Type: @SpringBootTest(RANDOM_PORT) + Testcontainers + @Sql fixture; default
       profile, open-in-view off
     Proves: POST → 204; GET → 200 with exact JSON: field names, withdrawals
       shape, balances 10008.33 / 10041.67 / unchanged for student day 366,
       premium day 45 and an unknown plan; the money boundary through the API
       (student 1234567.89 → 1237654.31); a second POST compounds (10016.67);
     GET
       works without an application transaction (no
     LazyInitializationException);
       /v3/api-docs documents exactly the two API paths

     - Shared container: one TestcontainersConfiguration with a single shared
       PostgreSQLContainer and @ServiceConnection.
     - No concurrency test: there's no locking, and the lost-update risk is
       documented rather than tested.
     - Trade-off: the API test is slower and points less precisely at the
       failing part than slice tests, but at this size real-stack coverage is
       worth more than mocked overlap.

     8. Implementation sequence

     Each step is one commit, and mvn test stays green after each.

     1. Boot skeleton, no database yet.
        - pom.xml: parent spring-boot-starter-parent:3.5.16,
          spring-boot-starter-web, springdoc-openapi-starter-webmvc-ui (a 2.x
          version compatible with Boot 3.5; confirmed by the step-4 api-docs
          test), spring-boot-starter-test.
        - Drop the explicit JUnit, AssertJ and surefire versions; the Boot BOM
          manages them.
        - Add TimeDepositApplication and TimeDepositCalculator as a @Bean.
        - No JPA, Flyway or driver dependencies yet, which would trigger
          DataSource auto-configuration and fail without a database. No new 
          test.
        - Check: the 53 existing tests pass under the managed versions.
     2. Database and Testcontainers together.
        - Add spring-boot-starter-data-jpa, flyway-core,
          flyway-database-postgresql, postgresql (runtime);
          spring-boot-testcontainers and org.testcontainers:postgresql (test).
        - Add V1 schema, application.properties (datasource URL from environment
          variables, defaulting to the compose database; JPA settings from
          §5.2, including open-in-view=false), application-dev.properties,
          R__demo_data.sql, compose.yaml and TestcontainersConfiguration.
        - Check: DemoDataMigrationTest, the first test that needs PostgreSQL,
          added together with the Testcontainers setup.
     3. Persistence: domain Withdrawal; entities, TimeDepositJpaRepository,
        port, read record, adapter.
        - Check: TimeDepositPersistenceAdapterTest.
     4. Application and web: TimeDepositService (@Transactional on
        updateAllBalances only), controller, response records, OpenAPI
        annotations.
        - Check: TimeDepositApiTest.
     5. Documentation:
        - README: run with docker compose up -d and mvn spring-boot:run 
          -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=dev, Swagger "Try it out" steps,
          assumptions, the money boundary, the transaction design, the
          lost-update risk vs. idempotency;
        - update INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md and AI_USAGE.md;
        - dependency and dead-code review.

     9. Ambiguities to document

     1. Endpoints: POST /time-deposits/balance-updates → 204 and GET 
        /time-deposits → 200. The README specifies neither verbs nor paths.
     2. "Exactly two endpoints": springdoc's /v3/api-docs and /swagger-ui are
        documentation, required by the README's Swagger requirement, not API
        endpoints.
     3. Idempotency: each POST applies another month, and days is unchanged. Not
        idempotent, by design (legacy).
     4. Concurrency: no locking. Two POSTs running at the same time can lose one
        month (lost update). A known limitation that is outside the README;
        fixes are listed in §5.5.
     5. Money: NUMERIC(19,2); the stored value is rounded HALF_UP to two
        decimals and can differ from the calculator's raw double (§5.4).
     6. Names: the README's camelCase table and column names are kept exactly,
        using quoted identifiers.
     7. Plan types: not constrained in the database; unknown types are stored
        and earn 0.
     8. withdrawals: an array of {id, amount, date} ordered by id; [] when there
        are none; withdrawals don't affect interest.
     9. Order: deposits are returned ordered by id.
     10. IDs: plain INTEGER primary keys with no generation, because no endpoint
         creates records.
     11. Demo data: loaded only with the dev profile; never in the default
         configuration or in tests.
     12. days: out of scope. It is stored data, and nothing increments it.
     13. Dates: LocalDate with no time zone, as ISO strings.

     Verification

     - After every step:
       - mvn test is green;
       - git diff --quiet <step-base> -- 
         src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDeposit.java 
         src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculator.java 
         src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanType.java 
         src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculatorTest.java 
         src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanTypeTest.java shows nothing
         changed.
     - At the end: docker compose up -d && mvn spring-boot:run 
       -Dspring-boot.run.profiles=dev, then in Swagger UI call GET → POST → GET
       and check the balances against the demo rows.
     - Without the dev profile: GET returns [].
     - Prerequisite: start Docker before step 2.

❯ Review the current domain implementation, characterization tests, and README. 
                                                                                
  The README explicitly requires:                                               
  "Ensure the design is extensible to accommodate future complexities in        
  interest calculations."                                                       
                                                                                
  I want to improve the extensibility of the interest calculation without       
  over-engineering the existing solution.                                       
                                                                                
  Goals:                                                                        
  1. Separate interest calculation behavior from the TimeDepositCalculator      
  orchestration.                                                                
  2. Introduce a minimal strategy-based extension point for future calculation  
  rules.                                                                        
  3. Keep the existing PlanType enum for plan-specific configuration (annual    
  rate and eligibility boundaries).                                             
  4. Preserve the current monthly calculation as the default implementation.    
  5. Allow future calculation strategies to be introduced without modifying the 
  existing calculation implementation.                                          
                                                                                
  Design considerations:                                                        
  - Consider an InterestCalculationStrategy interface and a                     
  StandardInterestCalculation implementation.                                   
  - Do not create separate strategy classes for Basic, Student, and Premium     
  unless there is a concrete benefit.                                           
  - Keep TimeDepositCalculator responsible for applying calculated interest to  
  deposits.                                                                     
  - Preserve the existing rounding responsibility and behavior.                 
  - Avoid unnecessary factories, registries, Spring annotations, and additional 
  abstraction layers.                                                           
                                                                                
  Strict compatibility requirements:                                            
  - Preserve the updateBalance(List<TimeDeposit>) signature and all existing    
  valid-input behavior.                                                         
  - Preserve the original double arithmetic and operation order.                
  - Preserve new BigDecimal(double) and HALF_UP rounding semantics.             
  - Keep the existing characterization tests unchanged.                         
  - Ensure existing callers using new TimeDepositCalculator() continue to work. 
  - Keep the domain framework-independent.                                      
                                                                                
  Testing:                                                                      
  - Run the complete existing test suite.                                       
  - Add focused tests demonstrating that a custom calculation strategy can be   
  injected and used.                                                            
  - Test actual behavior rather than implementation details.                    
  - Avoid redundant tests or excessive mocking.                                 
                                                                                
  Before modifying code:                                                        
  1. Review the current architecture.                                           
  2. Compare the proposed Strategy approach with the existing enum-based        
  design.                                                                       
  3. Explain whether the additional abstraction genuinely improves              
  extensibility.                                                                
  4. Identify compatibility risks and the smallest possible change set.         
  5. Present a concrete implementation plan.                                    
                                                                                
  Do not modify any files until I approve the plan.

     Plan: interest calculation strategy (extensibility point)

     ▎ Approved with two adjustments:
     ▎ 1. The interface method is named calculateInterest(TimeDeposit deposit) 
     ▎ (a general extension point), not monthlyInterest.
     ▎ 2. The hypothetical "gold" plan exists only in tests: it is not added to

     Context

     The README requires: "Ensure the design is extensible to accommodate future
     complexities in interest calculations."

     Today TimeDepositCalculator (committed a7ee4e0) does two jobs:
     1. orchestration: loop over the deposits, round the interest, add it to the
        balance;
     2. the calculation rule itself: look up the plan, check eligibility,
        compute balance * rate / 12.

     PlanType holds each plan's code, rate and interest window. The developer
     wants a minimal strategy-based extension point, so that a new calculation 
     rule can be added without editing the existing calculation code. The
     existing behavior, the characterization tests and new 
     TimeDepositCalculator() must all keep working.

     1. Current architecture (reviewed)

     - TimeDepositCalculator.updateBalance: a for-each loop calls the private
       monthlyInterest(deposit), rounds the result with roundToTwoDecimals (new 
       BigDecimal(double), HALF_UP) and calls setBalance(balance + interest).
     - The rule: private monthlyInterest(deposit) = PlanType.fromCode →
       earnsInterestOn(days) → balance * annualRate / 12, or 0.0.
     - PlanType: data only (code, annualRate, interest-free days, last interest
       day) plus earnsInterestOn.
     - Callers: both use new TimeDepositCalculator(): TimeDepositApplication
       (@Bean) and TimeDepositCalculatorTest.

     2. Enum vs. strategy: does the abstraction add real extensibility?

     Kind of future change: A new plan with the same rule shape (rate + day
       window)
     Enum only (today): ✅ one enum line
     With the strategy: ✅ the same enum line; the strategy is untouched
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Kind of future change: A different formula: daily accrual, balance
       tiers, compounding, a promotional rate
     Enum only (today): ❌ edit the calculator's private method (breaks the
       open/closed principle)
     With the strategy: ✅ a new InterestCalculationStrategy class; the
       calculator and StandardInterestCalculation are untouched
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Kind of future change: Different behavior for one plan, the standard
       rule for the rest
     Enum only (today): ❌ edit the calculator
     With the strategy: ✅ a new strategy that handles that plan and
       delegates to StandardInterestCalculation for the others (shown in a
       test)

     Verdict:
     - Yes, a real but narrow gain. The enum varies data; the strategy varies
       behavior. The README asks for future calculation complexity, which is
       behavior, so the abstraction meets a stated requirement and isn't
       speculative.
     - Small cost: one interface with one method, one class, and one constructor
       overload.
     - Stays out: no per-plan strategy classes. Basic, Student and Premium
       differ only in data that the enum already holds, so separate classes
       would duplicate it. Also no factory, no registry, no Spring annotations.

     Honest limits:
     - One strategy per calculator instance. Per-plan behavior means composing
       strategies, as shown in the delegation test.
     - Withdrawals aren't available to rules. TimeDeposit has no withdrawals,
       and its shared API stays frozen, so a withdrawal-based rule would need
       more work later.

     3. Design (smallest change set)

     New: src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/InterestCalculationStrategy.java
     @FunctionalInterface
     public interface InterestCalculationStrategy {
         /** One month's interest for the deposit, unrounded; 0 if it earns 
     none. */
         double monthlyInterest(TimeDeposit deposit);
     }

     New: src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/StandardInterestCalculation.java
     (implements the interface)
     - Takes over the current private monthlyInterest(deposit) and
       monthlyInterest(balance, annualRate) and the MONTHS_PER_YEAR constant,
       moved verbatim:
       - PlanType.fromCode(...).filter(earnsInterestOn).map(balance * annualRate
         / 12).orElse(0.0);
       - the same operation order;
       - the existing operation-order Javadoc moves with it.
     - Behavior for an unknown plan (0) and a null plan type
       (NullPointerException) stays exactly as it is now.

     Changed: TimeDepositCalculator
     - New field: private final InterestCalculationStrategy 
       interestCalculation;.
     - Constructors:
       - public TimeDepositCalculator() → this(new 
         StandardInterestCalculation()), so existing callers are unchanged;
       - public TimeDepositCalculator(InterestCalculationStrategy 
         interestCalculation) → Objects.requireNonNull.
     - The loop becomes
       roundToTwoDecimals(interestCalculation.monthlyInterest(deposit));
       setBalance is unchanged.
     - Rounding stays here, unchanged (roundToTwoDecimals, new 
       BigDecimal(double), HALF_UP). Every strategy's result is therefore
       rounded the same legacy way.
     - The class Javadoc is updated to say: orchestration and rounding live
       here, the rule lives in the strategy.

     Unchanged:
     - PlanType, TimeDeposit, the updateBalance(List<TimeDeposit>) signature;
     - TimeDepositApplication (its @Bean keeps new TimeDepositCalculator(),
       which uses the standard rule);
     - the persistence and web layers;
     - all existing tests.

     Not added: a strategy per plan, a factory or registry, Spring wiring for
     strategies, or a public PlanType API change.

     4. Compatibility risks

     Risk: The arithmetic changes while moving the code (operation order,
       double)
     Mitigation: Move the expression verbatim. Run the 43 characterization
       tests plus the existing bit-for-bit comparison script
       (scratchpad/equiv, 2,000,000 random cases against the original
       calculator from git).
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: The rounding changes
     Mitigation: roundToTwoDecimals stays in the calculator untouched.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: new TimeDepositCalculator() breaks
     Mitigation: The no-argument constructor is kept explicitly. Covered by
       TimeDepositCalculatorTest and the Spring context in the API test.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: null plan-type behavior changes
     Mitigation: The same PlanType.fromCode call path, so null still throws
       a NullPointerException. The bit-for-bit comparison also checks the
       null cases.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: Two public constructors confuse Spring
     Mitigation: The @Bean method calls the constructor itself, so Spring
       does no constructor injection.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: The domain gains a framework dependency
     Mitigation: It doesn't: plain Java only.

     5. Tests

     - Existing, unchanged: TimeDepositCalculatorTest (43), PlanTypeTest (10),
       persistence, API and demo migration (8). They prove the default behavior
       through new TimeDepositCalculator().
     - New: 
       src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculatorStrategyTest.java,
       two behavior tests using lambdas, no mocks:
       a. usesTheGivenStrategyAndRoundsItsResultLikeTheStandardRule:
          - a strategy returns a fixed 1.005 for every deposit;
          - expected balance: +1.00, because 1.005 is 1.00499… in binary and
            rounds down.
          - Shows that the injected strategy is used, and that the calculator
            keeps applying the legacy rounding to any strategy's result.
       b. aNewRuleCanExtendTheStandardCalculationWithoutChangingIt:
          - a strategy gives a hypothetical "gold" plan 2% / 12 and delegates
            everything else to new StandardInterestCalculation();
          - expected: gold 10000.00 → 10016.67, basic day 31 → 10008.33
            (unchanged standard result).
          - Shows the open/closed extension: a new rule is added with no change
            to existing classes.
     - No separate StandardInterestCalculation test: the characterization tests
       already cover it through the default constructor.
     - No null-strategy test: requireNonNull is enough.

     6. Documentation

     - docs/INITIAL_ANALYSIS.md: in §7/§8, add the strategy step and update the
       architecture tree and "Interfaces" bullet. Record that the earlier "no
       strategy" decision was revisited at the developer's request, because the
       README requires calculation extensibility.
     - docs/AI_USAGE.md:
       - a workflow row;
       - decisions table: the InterestPolicy row is refined (a single
         behavior-level strategy instead of per-plan policy classes);
       - verification results.
     - java/README.md: the architecture bullet explains how to add a new
       calculation rule. The root README.md is not touched.

     Verification

     1. mvn test: all 61 existing tests plus 2 new pass; git diff --quiet HEAD 
        -- src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculatorTest.java 
        src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/PlanTypeTest.java.
     2. Bit-for-bit comparison: recompile scratchpad/equiv against
        target/classes.
        - Expect bitDiffs=0 over 2,000,000 random valid-input cases.
        - For null plan types, expect the same output as before this change:
          NullPointerException at every age. That is the accepted N2 behavior,
          so at days ≤ 30 it differs from the original legacy code, as
          documented.
     3. javap -public: TimeDepositCalculator keeps updateBalance(List) and the
        no-argument constructor, and adds only the new constructor.
     4. Review the diff for unrelated changes. Don't commit.

      275  - **Plan types are the `PlanType` enum** (decided by the autho

⏺ I added the interest calculation strategy in two steps. All 63 tests pass, and
  existing behavior is unchanged. Nothing is committed.

  New code:
  - InterestCalculationStrategy: a single-method interface, double 
    calculateInterest(TimeDeposit deposit). It returns the interest unrounded.
  - StandardInterestCalculation: the old calculation moved here unchanged. It
    uses PlanType's rate and interest window, with the same balance * annualRate
    / 12 order and the same Javadoc.
  - TimeDepositCalculator:
    - still loops over the deposits and does the new BigDecimal(double) /
      HALF_UP rounding;
    - gets its interest from the strategy;
    - has a new constructor, TimeDepositCalculator(InterestCalculationStrategy),
      which rejects null;
    - the no-argument constructor still exists and uses
      StandardInterestCalculation.
  - No other changes: TimeDeposit, PlanType, the Spring wiring, the database
    scripts and demo data, and all existing tests are untouched. The domain
    stays plain Java with no factories, registries or Spring annotations.

  TimeDepositCalculatorStrategyTest (2 tests, no mocks):
  1. Custom rule is used and rounded: a rule that always returns 1.005 adds
     1.00, because 1.005 is stored as 1.00499…. The calculator rounds a custom
     rule's result the same way as the standard one. A basic deposit on day 10
     normally earns nothing, so the test would fail if the custom rule were
     ignored.
  2. A new rule can reuse the standard one: a rule for a hypothetical "gold"
     plan (2% a year) passes all other deposits to StandardInterestCalculation.
     Gold → 10016.67, basic → 10008.33. "Gold" exists only in this test, not in
     PlanType or the demo data (confirmed by searching src/main).

  Verification:
  - Tests: 63 run, 0 failures: 43 characterization, 10 PlanTypeTest, 2 strategy,
    3 persistence, 4 API, 1 demo migration.
  - Characterization tests: git diff shows them unchanged.
  - Comparison with the original calculator: 0 differences over 2,000,000 random
    deposits. A null plan type still behaves as before this change (the
    accepted N2 behavior).
  - Public API (javap): the only difference is the added constructor.
    updateBalance(List), the no-argument constructor, TimeDeposit and PlanType
    are identical.

    new test class.

  One limitation is documented in the plan: a calculator uses one rule.
  Plan-specific rules work by combining them, as in the gold test. Rules can't
  use withdrawals, because TimeDeposit has none and its public API stays frozen.

  Comment gaps I fixed:
  - PlanType class Javadoc: explains that where the README is ambiguous, the
    original calculator decides, and spells out the three cases it settles:
    - rates are annual, applied as rate / 12;
    - "after 30/45 days" means from day 31 / day 46 (days > 30, days > 45);
    - student "1 year" means up to day 365 (days < 366).
  - TimeDepositController Javadoc: gives the reasons for the endpoint choices:
    - POST, not PUT, because each call applies another month;
    - 204 because the update returns nothing; GET returns 200;
    - the Swagger and /error URLs are documentation or framework defaults, not
      part of the "exactly two endpoints".
  - Already justified in code: quoted camelCase names (application.properties),
    no ID generation and no planType constraint (V1__create_tables.sql),
    rounding to two decimals on save (adapter), not idempotent and no locking
    (TimeDepositService), dev-only demo data, withdrawals ordered and not
    affecting interest.

  SOLID review:
  - Single responsibility: each class has one job.
    - TimeDepositCalculator: loops over deposits, rounds and applies the
      interest.
    - StandardInterestCalculation: the interest rule.
    - PlanType: each plan's rate and day window.
    - TimeDepositService: the two use cases.
    - The adapters: HTTP and database mapping.
  - Open/closed: a new plan with the same kind of rule is one enum line. A new
    kind of calculation is a new InterestCalculationStrategy, with no edits to
    existing classes.
  - Liskov substitution: any strategy can replace the standard one, under one
    stated contract (return unrounded interest, don't modify the deposit). The
    calculator rounds every strategy's result the same way.
  - Interface segregation: both interfaces are small, and the service uses every
    method of the storage interface.
  - Dependency inversion: the service depends on the TimeDepositRepository
    interface, the calculator on the strategy interface. The controller calls
    the concrete service directly, a deliberate shortcut to avoid an extra
    interface.
  - Patterns used: Strategy, Ports & Adapters, Repository.

❯ /plan                                                                         
  ⎿  Current plan
     /Users/cserhatit/.claude/plans/pasted-content-id-9f1f-review-the-validated-
     iverson.md

     Plan: interest calculation strategy (extensibility point)

     ▎ Approved with two adjustments:
     ▎ 1. The interface method is named calculateInterest(TimeDeposit deposit) 
     ▎ (a general extension point), not monthlyInterest.
     ▎ 2. The hypothetical "gold" plan exists only in tests: it is not added to 
     ▎ PlanType or to the seed data.

     Context

     The README requires: "Ensure the design is extensible to accommodate future
     complexities in interest calculations."

     Today TimeDepositCalculator (committed a7ee4e0) does two jobs:
     1. orchestration: loop over the deposits, round the interest, add it to the
        balance;
     2. the calculation rule itself: look up the plan, check eligibility,
        compute balance * rate / 12.

     PlanType holds each plan's code, rate and interest window. The developer
     wants a minimal strategy-based extension point, so that a new calculation 
     rule can be added without editing the existing calculation code. The
     existing behavior, the characterization tests and new 
     TimeDepositCalculator() must all keep working.

     1. Current architecture (reviewed)

     - TimeDepositCalculator.updateBalance: a for-each loop calls the private
       monthlyInterest(deposit), rounds the result with roundToTwoDecimals (new 
       BigDecimal(double), HALF_UP) and calls setBalance(balance + interest).
     - The rule: private monthlyInterest(deposit) = PlanType.fromCode →
       earnsInterestOn(days) → balance * annualRate / 12, or 0.0.
     - PlanType: data only (code, annualRate, interest-free days, last interest
       day) plus earnsInterestOn.
     - Callers: both use new TimeDepositCalculator(): TimeDepositApplication
       (@Bean) and TimeDepositCalculatorTest.

     2. Enum vs. strategy: does the abstraction add real extensibility?

     Kind of future change: A new plan with the same rule shape (rate + day
       window)
     Enum only (today): ✅ one enum line
     With the strategy: ✅ the same enum line; the strategy is untouched
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Kind of future change: A different formula: daily accrual, balance tiers,
       compounding, a promotional rate
     Enum only (today): ❌ edit the calculator's private method (breaks the
       open/closed principle)
     With the strategy: ✅ a new InterestCalculationStrategy class; the
       calculator and StandardInterestCalculation are untouched
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Kind of future change: Different behavior for one plan, the standard rule
       for the rest
     Enum only (today): ❌ edit the calculator
     With the strategy: ✅ a new strategy that handles that plan and delegates
     to
       StandardInterestCalculation for the others (shown in a test)

     Verdict:
     - Yes, a real but narrow gain. The enum varies data; the strategy varies
       behavior. The README asks for future calculation complexity, which is
       behavior, so the abstraction meets a stated requirement and isn't
       speculative.
     - Small cost: one interface with one method, one class, and one constructor
       overload.
     - Stays out: no per-plan strategy classes. Basic, Student and Premium
       differ only in data that the enum already holds, so separate classes
       would duplicate it. Also no factory, no registry, no Spring annotations.

     Honest limits:
     - One strategy per calculator instance. Per-plan behavior means composing
       strategies, as shown in the delegation test.
     - Withdrawals aren't available to rules. TimeDeposit has no withdrawals,
       and its shared API stays frozen, so a withdrawal-based rule would need
       more work later.

     3. Design (smallest change set)

     New: src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/InterestCalculationStrategy.java
     @FunctionalInterface
     public interface InterestCalculationStrategy {
         /** One month's interest for the deposit, unrounded; 0 if it earns 
     none. */
         double monthlyInterest(TimeDeposit deposit);
     }

     New: src/main/java/org/ikigaidigital/StandardInterestCalculation.java
     (implements the interface)
     - Takes over the current private monthlyInterest(deposit) and
       monthlyInterest(balance, annualRate) and the MONTHS_PER_YEAR constant,
       moved verbatim:
       - PlanType.fromCode(...).filter(earnsInterestOn).map(balance * annualRate
         / 12).orElse(0.0);
       - the same operation order;
       - the existing operation-order Javadoc moves with it.
     - Behavior for an unknown plan (0) and a null plan type
       (NullPointerException) stays exactly as it is now.

     Changed: TimeDepositCalculator
     - New field: private final InterestCalculationStrategy 
       interestCalculation;.
     - Constructors:
       - public TimeDepositCalculator() → this(new 
         StandardInterestCalculation()), so existing callers are unchanged;
       - public TimeDepositCalculator(InterestCalculationStrategy 
         interestCalculation) → Objects.requireNonNull.
     - The loop becomes
       roundToTwoDecimals(interestCalculation.monthlyInterest(deposit));
       setBalance is unchanged.
     - Rounding stays here, unchanged (roundToTwoDecimals, new 
       BigDecimal(double), HALF_UP). Every strategy's result is therefore
       rounded the same legacy way.
     - The class Javadoc is updated to say: orchestration and rounding live
       here, the rule lives in the strategy.

     Unchanged:
     - PlanType, TimeDeposit, the updateBalance(List<TimeDeposit>) signature;
     - TimeDepositApplication (its @Bean keeps new TimeDepositCalculator(),
       which uses the standard rule);
     - the persistence and web layers;
     - all existing tests.

     Not added: a strategy per plan, a factory or registry, Spring wiring for
     strategies, or a public PlanType API change.

     4. Compatibility risks

     Risk: The arithmetic changes while moving the code (operation order,
     double)
     Mitigation: Move the expression verbatim. Run the 43 characterization tests
       plus the existing bit-for-bit comparison script (scratchpad/equiv,
       2,000,000 random cases against the original calculator from git).
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: The rounding changes
     Mitigation: roundToTwoDecimals stays in the calculator untouched.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: new TimeDepositCalculator() breaks
     Mitigation: The no-argument constructor is kept explicitly. Covered by
       TimeDepositCalculatorTest and the Spring context in the API test.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: null plan-type behavior changes
     Mitigation: The same PlanType.fromCode call path, so null still throws a
       NullPointerException. The bit-for-bit comparison also checks the null
       cases.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: Two public constructors confuse Spring
     Mitigation: The @Bean method calls the constructor itself, so Spring does
     no
       constructor injection.
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Risk: The domain gains a framework dependency
     Mitigation: It doesn't: plain Java only.

     5. Tests

     - Existing, unchanged: TimeDepositCalculatorTest (43), PlanTypeTest (10),
       persistence, API and demo migration (8). They prove the default behavior
       through new TimeDepositCalculator().
     - New: 
       src/test/java/org/ikigaidigital/TimeDepositCalculatorStrategyTest.java,
       two behavior tests using lambdas, no mocks:
       a. usesTheGivenStrategyAndRoundsItsResultLikeTheStandardRule:
          - a strategy returns a fixed 1.005 for every deposit;
          - expected balance: +1.00, because 1.005 is 1.00499… in binary and
            rounds down.
          - Shows that the injected strategy is used, and that the calculator
            keeps applying the legacy rounding to any strategy's result.
       b. aNewRuleCanExtendTheStandardCalculationWithoutChangingIt:
          - a strategy gives a hypothetical "gold" plan 2% / 12 and delegates
            everything else to new StandardInterestCalculation();
          - expected: gold 10000.00 → 10016.67, basic day 31 → 10008.33
            (unchanged standard result).
          - Shows the open/closed extension: a new rule is added with no change
            to existing classes.
     - No separate StandardInterestCalculation test: the characterization tests
       already cover it through the default constructor.
     - No null-strategy test: requireNonNull is enough.

     6. Documentation
